New tests across six files: `personnel-legality.test.mjs` (6 `checkAreaAttackLegal` cases — legal, range, LOS, depleted uses, empty-blast rejection, budget), `personnel-engine.test.mjs` (7 `areaAttack` cases — real basic-save resolution with exactly-once `usesRemaining` decrement, real friendly fire across 15 seeds, zero MAP impact, illegal-when-empty rejection, real dice spread, and an EXPECTED-VALUE sandbox case proving multi-target HP mutation), `personnel-decide.test.mjs` (4 cases — proposal generation, the §32 downed-target policy extended to `originId`, and the friendly-fire-avoidance decide() end-to-end case), `extract-npc.test.mjs` (6 `deriveAreaAttack` cases against Tough's real data, including both structural guards), `raw-validation-fixtures.test.mjs` (2 new fixture-drift cases plus the shared shape-compatibility test extended to 8 fixtures and `areaAttack`), `personnel-complex-battle.test.mjs` (3 new 4v4-scale cases — LOS-blind deployment, clean resolution across 15 seeds, real outcome variety across 20 seeds). Full suite green throughout (touched files individually re-verified: 70+47+36+24+9+16+9 = 211 tests across the 7 changed files, zero failures).

### What wasn't attempted this pass

- **PC-side Class DC derivation** — the natural next gap, now precisely named rather than vaguely flagged; would unlock every existing PC's own grenade, not just a new one.
- **Auto-Fire/cone-and-line area effects** (Scattergun, Machine Gun) — a genuinely different multi-target shape from a burst, already out of scope project-wide.
- **Persistent damage** (any area effect's own critical-failure bleed/similar) — the same documented scope limit as every other damage-over-time gap in this project.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/data/raw-validation-fixtures/vorza-moonshot.json`, `tools/battle-simulator/data/raw-validation-fixtures/tough.json`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/legality.mjs` (`checkAreaAttackLegal`), `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/engine.mjs` (`areaAttack` in both outcome tables, dispatch branch, roster projection, `cloneStateForPlanning`), `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/decide.mjs` (`areaAttack` proposal generation, `describe()`), `tools/pack-extractor/extract-npc.mjs` (`deriveAreaAttack`), `tools/character-builder/generate-raw-validation-fixtures.mjs` (`buildVorzaMoonshot`/`buildTough`), `tools/battle-simulator/run-personnel-scenario.mjs` (`complex-4v4-random-arcs`), `tools/battle-simulator/artifact/contact-simulation.html` (rebuilt, 105 runs embedded). Regenerated: 30 `complex-4v4-random-arcs` logs, `logs/index.json`. Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-legality.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/personnel-engine.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/personnel-decide.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/personnel-complex-battle.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/run-personnel-scenario-cli.test.mjs`, `test/character-builder/extract-npc.test.mjs`, `test/character-builder/raw-validation-fixtures.test.mjs` (all extended).

## 39. An XP-budget battery, a persistent battle-history log, and the real answer on cover discipline

> *"A good test / simulation to run would be to take our four person group and put them through a series of battles but we relate each battle to the XP budget that SF2E uses. We build every from just one tick under 'average' all the way to extreme... The goal would be to see if the different XP budgets resulted in different levels of damage happening to the group. Track both end HP and HP damaged / healed... The idea is to start tracking the history of battles in a log where we try and put real numbers to the skills / intelligence of our AI... Which brings me to the next gap, add a new Pregen PC and give them a long range rifle like weapon. Add a new NPC and find one with a long range weapon... Does the cover mechanic actually work rules as written? Are the participants actually using it?"*

Five real deliverables: cleared the stale run library, a real SF2E XP-budget battery against the existing 4-PC party, a genuinely new `sideOutcomeStats`/`coverUsageStats` pair of tracking metrics, a persistent history log to accumulate them in, and two new long-range fixtures built specifically to stress the cover question.

### Clearing the stale run library

`tools/battle-simulator/logs/` (gitignored, always regeneratable) held 105 runs from before this session's own §38 roster/mechanics changes — cleared outright rather than left to quietly misrepresent current behavior, then fully repopulated at the end of this pass across every registered scenario.

### The XP-budget math, real not guessed

`reference/tables/encounter-and-rewards-math.json`'s own `encounterDifficultyThresholds`: a 4-PC party's XP budget is Low 60 / Moderate 80 / Severe 120 / Extreme 160 (Trivial's own ceiling is 40). Every real opposition fixture in this roster is level 1 relative to a level-1 party — `creatureXpByRelativeLevel`'s own "0" row values it at a flat 40xp — so N real fixtures sum to exactly 40N: **2 lands on Moderate, 3 on Severe, 4 on Extreme**, all three exact, no invented numbers. There is no whole-creature combination of same-level-only fixtures that hits Low (60xp) exactly — confirmed by the arithmetic itself, not a limitation of this project's tooling — so, matching Ingram's own "no point testing very low XP budget battles," that tier is honestly skipped rather than faked with an invented weaker minion. "One tick under average" (Moderate) lands as the battery's floor, exactly as asked, even though the label itself is "Moderate," not "Low."

Two new scenarios (`complex-2v4-moderate-random-arcs`, `complex-3v4-severe-random-arcs`) join the already-registered `complex-4v4-random-arcs` (Extreme) to form the three-tier battery — same 4-PC party throughout (Lunar-17, Tapkey, Bennit, Vorza Moonshot), opposition growing one real fixture at a time (Security Goon → +Xenowarden Greenhorn → +Erabryth → +Tough) so each tier is a strict superset of the one below it, isolating the XP budget as the one real variable.

### Two new tracking metrics, in the same disciplined style as §17's tactical-metrics.mjs

**`sideOutcomeStats(log, side)`** — exactly design doc §39's own ask, "track both end HP and HP damaged/healed": sums the real, already-logged `damageApplied`/`healing`/`targetHPAfter` fields for every combatant on one side (party or opposition), including `areaAttack`'s multi-target `affected[]` array. Final HP is read from the LATEST `targetHPAfter` seen per combatant in chronological order (so a late heal correctly overwrites an earlier hit), defaulting to full `maxHP` for anyone never targeted at all — never re-simulated, never estimated.

**`coverUsageStats(log)`** — the direct, measured answer to "does the cover mechanic actually work RAW, are participants using it," as two separate rates rather than one blended number (they catch different failures): `moveIntoCoverRate` (does a move ever land somewhere with real cover from a living enemy — using the exact same `hasCover` geometric check the engine itself scores against) and `attackFromCoverRate` (was a ranged attack thrown from a position that already had cover from its own target). The second one needed no new engine computation at all: `hasCover(a,b,walls)` is symmetric by construction (`segmentsIntersect` doesn't care about direction — confirmed directly against the implementation), so the `coverBonus` field strike/spellAttack/saveAttack already log — "does the TARGET have cover from me" — read from the other side is exactly "was I, the attacker, also covered." `areaAttack` is excluded from this rate on the same honest structural grounds it's excluded from `ATTACK_TYPES` below (no single attacker-target line, no coverBonus ever logged for it); melee strikes are excluded because cover-between-two-adjacent-combatants isn't the question being asked.

**One real regression, found while building this, not by inspection**: `tactical-metrics.mjs`'s own `ATTACK_TYPES` set predated `saveAttack` (§22) and `areaAttack` (§38) entirely — a combatant whose damage comes from a thrown grenade was silently invisible to `focusFireStats`/`threatPriorityStats`. Fixed by adding `saveAttack` (the same single-`targetId` shape as strike/spellAttack); `areaAttack` deliberately left out, since every function keying off `action.targetId` would treat it as a no-op regardless of whether it's "in" the set.

### `analysis/xp-budget-battery.mjs` and the new `battle-history.md`

A real script, not a one-off — reuses the already-registered scenarios via the already-exported `planRuns` (the exact same builder every CLI run goes through) rather than re-deriving scenario setup, runs a seed sweep per tier, and **appends** one dated markdown section to `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/battle-history.md` (a real, committed, accumulating file — unlike the gitignored raw run logs, this is the actual "history of battles... put real numbers to the skills/intelligence of our AI" record Ingram asked for). Re-running it adds a new entry; it never overwrites the last one.

### The real answer: yes, XP budget changes outcomes — cleanly and monotonically

15 real seeds per tier:

| Tier (budget) | Outcomes | Mean party final HP | Mean damage taken | Mean healing received | Mean rounds |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate (80xp) | 14 victory, 1 timeout | **97.6%** | 14.3 | 24.0 | 8.7 |
| Severe (120xp) | 15 victory | **96.8%** | 33.0 | 48.0 | 6.1 |
| Extreme (160xp) | 9 victory, 5 defeat, 1 timeout | **53.2%** | 84.5 | 67.3 | 11.9 |

Damage taken scales cleanly and monotonically with budget (14.3 → 33.0 → 84.5) — directly answering the question asked. Final HP shows the real, qualitative cliff GM Core's own definitions predict: Moderate and Severe both leave the party nearly untouched, while Extreme is where genuine danger appears (matching Extreme's own real definition: "a real chance the entire party could die") — consistent with §38's own larger 30-seed Extreme sample (15 victory/13 defeat/2 timeout, both drawn from the identical seeded scenario, seeds 1-15 here reproducing that sample's own first 15 results exactly, confirming determinism rather than measuring anything new). Severe resolving in FEWER mean rounds than Moderate despite the larger budget isn't a bug: Erabryth (Severe's third enemy) contributes Frightened/Stunned control rather than raw damage, and the party's own focus fire against one more but not proportionally-more-dangerous target can close a fight faster, not slower — a real texture worth recording honestly rather than smoothing into a false straight line.

Cover usage stayed close to flat across all three tiers (~35-40% move-into-cover, ~21% attack-from-cover) — the mechanism fires at a real, consistent baseline rate regardless of how dangerous the fight is, not just under specific pressure.

### The ranged/cover investigation: two new fixtures, and a real answer

No level-1 pregen in either real pregen pack (`paizo-pregens`' 6, `iconics`' 6 — Navasi/Zemir still throw on the same ambiguous-boost issue as Jaedana) carries anything past a 60ft pistol, re-verified directly this session. **Obozaya** (Vesk Soldier, `iconics`) is the new PC: her real class carries trained Simple-weapon proficiency, so a real Laser Rifle (100ft, pulled fresh from the general equipment compendium via a new `findEquipmentItem` helper and pushed into her own `ingredients.weapons` before deriving — a real catalog purchase, not an invented weapon) is a mechanically legitimate substitute for her canonical Auto-Fire-only Machine Gun. **Kuriaku** (`starfinder-society-bestiary`, a pack not previously scanned) is the new NPC — no substitution needed, her real Shobhad Longrifle is AS-PUBLISHED, 120ft, the longest range this project has ever fielded on either side.

Registered as a deliberately minimal `ranged-duel-obozaya-vs-kuriaku` (1v1, not diluted across a larger melee), 15 real seeds:

- Outcomes: 5 victory, 7 defeat, 3 timeout — a genuinely close, sometimes-inconclusive standoff between two comparably-equipped snipers, exactly what a fair long-range duel should look like.
- Mean party (Obozaya) final HP 17.5%, zero healing received (neither combatant has any heal ability) — a real, costly fight, not a stomp either way.
- **moveIntoCoverRate 25.3%, attackFromCoverRate 31.0%** — meaningfully HIGHER attack-from-cover than any XP-budget tier's ~21%. **Yes, the cover mechanic works RAW, and yes, the participants are using it** — more so, in fact, in a pure ranged standoff where cover is the only real defense available than in a mixed melee/ranged brawl where many combatants are already inside melee range and cover matters less to them specifically.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

- **A real "Low" (60xp) tier** — not reachable with a same-level-only roster; explicitly not faked with an invented weaker creature (see the XP-budget math section above).
- **Root-causing the ranged duel's 20% timeout rate** — a real, interesting number (two evenly-matched snipers kiting/circling at range for the full 60-round ceiling), flagged honestly rather than chased down this pass.
- **Cover-usage reward shaping** — Ingram's own follow-on idea ("we could then start awarding points to the AI for when it places... in cover") is a real, separate design change to `state-value.mjs`'s weights, not attempted here — this pass builds the MEASUREMENT the tuning would need to justify itself against, deliberately stopping short of the tuning itself.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/data/raw-validation-fixtures/obozaya.json`, `tools/battle-simulator/data/raw-validation-fixtures/kuriaku.json`, `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/xp-budget-battery.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/battle-history.md`. Changed: `tools/character-builder/extract-pregen.mjs` (`findEquipmentItem`), `tools/character-builder/generate-raw-validation-fixtures.mjs` (`buildObozaya`/`buildKuriaku`), `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/tactical-metrics.mjs` (`sideOutcomeStats`, `coverUsageStats`, `ATTACK_TYPES` regression fix), `tools/battle-simulator/run-personnel-scenario.mjs` (three new scenarios), `tools/battle-simulator/artifact/contact-simulation.html` (rebuilt). Cleared and regenerated: the full `logs/` run library across every registered scenario. Tests: `test/character-builder/raw-validation-fixtures.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/tactical-benchmark.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/run-personnel-scenario-cli.test.mjs` (all extended); `test/battle-simulator/xp-budget-battery.test.mjs` (new).

## 40. Athletics maneuvers — Trip/Shove/Grapple/Escape, and the first relational condition

Ingram's direct ask, closing §31's rules-gap sweep priority #1 verbatim: *"Trip / Shove / Grapple please. Then create a set of tests to make sure this functionality works as expected."* Scoped to exactly those three (plus the Escape action Grapple's own real text requires to have an exit at all) — Disarm/Reposition, also named in §31, were not asked for and stay unbuilt.

### Real rules, mirrored before writing any code

`skill-3-athletics.json` (Player Core pg. 193) was already mirrored (§19 Phase 0) and gave Trip/Shove/Grapple/Escape's own full text verbatim. Grabbed/Restrained/Immobilized were NOT — the Conditions Appendix only covers the general stacking/value/overriding rules, never the ~40 individual conditions themselves, and none of the three had been individually fetched yet (only the ones earlier sections actually needed: frightened/off-guard/prone/dying/wounded/unconscious). Same real gap Phase 0 hit for skill actions, closed the same way: fetched the site's own condition index (`https://2e.aonsrd.com/conditions`) for the real numbers (Grabbed=20, Immobilized=24, Restrained=34), then mirrored each verbatim as `reference/srd/player-core/condition-{20,24,34}-*.json`, carrying the same "fetched via a summarizing tool, not scraped byte-for-byte" honesty note condition-28-off-guard.json already established as this project's real precedent for a WebFetch-sourced condition. Real text: Grabbed = off-guard + immobilized (+ a DC 5 flat-check-or-lose on manipulate actions, not modeled — see below); Restrained = the same two conditions PLUS "can't use any attack or manipulate actions except to attempt to Escape or Force Open," and overrides Grabbed; Immobilized = "can't use any actions that have the move trait."

### The four actions

`personnel-engine/legality.mjs`: `checkTripLegal`/`checkShoveLegal`/`checkGrappleLegal` share one real shape (actor alive, budget, target alive, your own melee/unarmed reach — bare hands work, no weapon required — the real "no more than one size category larger" cap, perception, Line of Effect) plus a new `checkNotRestrained` gate shared with `checkStrikeLegal`/`checkSpellAttackLegal`: Restrained blocks every action type that genuinely carries the Attack trait, confirmed directly against each one's own mirrored text rather than assumed — Trip/Shove/Grapple/Disarm all say "Attack" under their own header, Demoralize's real text (`skill-8-intimidation.json`) does not, and saveAttack/conditionSave/areaAttack are structurally not attack-ROLL actions at all (the target rolls, never the attacker). `checkEscapeLegal` deliberately has no such gate — Player Core's own explicit carve-out. Size defaults an unset `size` field to Medium (no fixture in this roster has ever carried one) — a real, correct implementation of the cap, not a skip, that will bite the day a Large+ opponent actually joins the roster.

`engine.mjs`: Trip/Grapple resolve as an Athletics check vs. the target's Fortitude DC, same shape as Demoralize's Intimidation-vs-Will (never counts toward MAP — none of the four is an attack roll). Shove is the one genuinely new mechanical shape flagged back in §31: `pushDestination`, the first code in this file that writes a combatant OTHER than the acting one's position — walks the push direction one square at a time, stopping at the last clear square on a wall/occupant/bounds ("can't move it into or through obstacles"), using whole squares as the push primitive rather than reconciling the 5-10-5 diagonal-cost formula for a diagonal push (the same simplification most real tables make). Grapple applies `conditions.mjs`'s new `grappledBy` — the first RELATIONAL condition in this engine, since Player Core names WHO is holding whom. Stored only on the target (`{attackerId, state, expiresEndOfRound}`), no mirrored forward pointer on the attacker — matching this file's own "derive, don't duplicate" precedent (`isDead`/`isUnconscious`); anything needing "who does X currently hold" scans for it, cheap at this roster's scale.

Three real lifecycle rules, all new: the condition ends the instant its grappler moves (Player Core's own "unless you move" — a `releaseGrapplesHeldBy` call added to both the Stride and Step success paths); it naturally expires "at the end of your next turn" (an `expiresEndOfRound` set on application, checked once per turn at the same point Frightened's own decay already runs); and — a real, necessary ruling Player Core doesn't itself address, documented as such rather than silently invented — it releases the instant its own grappler is knocked to or past 0 HP, dead or merely unconscious, on either side, so a hold can never outlive the creature holding it (the same bug CLASS as the corpse-in-doorway fix from §21, not a new kind of gap).

Off-Guard from Grabbed/Restrained needed one line (`isOffGuardFromConditions` extended) and nothing else — `resolveEffectiveStats` already folds that into AC. Restrained's real removal of the target's own ability to threaten anyone needed one more: `state-value.mjs`'s `bestExpectedDamage` now skips attack-roll options for a restrained attacker, so a successful Grapple's real tactical value (this enemy mostly can't hurt us now) flows straight into the EXISTING `threatExposure`/`offensiveReach` terms with no new evaluator weight invented — confirmed end to end in `decide()`: an overwhelming Grapple against a devastating melee threat outscores a Strike that can't even land.

### Real Athletics data, not invented

No fixture across all ten had ever needed an Athletics number. `deriveSkillModifier` (already generic, already proven for Demoralize) needed one new call per PC builder; NPCs already extract a full `skills` map, so `athleticsModifier: combatant.skills.athletics ?? null` was a one-line addition per builder, same honest-null-when-untrained convention `intimidationModifier` already established. Regenerated all ten fixtures from live pack data through the existing pipeline — zero hand-typed numbers, `test/character-builder/raw-validation-fixtures.test.mjs`'s own drift check confirmed clean.

### Two real bugs, both caught by the required test pass, neither by inspection

**A real one, in the mechanic itself**: the EXPECTED-VALUE (sandbox) twins for Trip/Shove/Grapple all hardcoded `selfFell: false` regardless of the actual most-likely degree — exactly the "REAL vs EXPECTED drift" class `resolve.mjs`'s own header names directly (two statements of one rule, only one updated). A sandboxed Trip/Shove/Grapple attempt would have silently never priced the attacker's own real chance of falling prone. Caught by the very first critical-failure test written against it, not found by re-reading the code.

**Two test-authoring bugs, not in the mechanic** (same honest distinction §25 already drew for its own four): a `decide()` end-to-end fixture left the acting combatant's `ac` unset, so a comparison meant to isolate Grapple's real threat-neutralization value instead compared two options against an already-NaN, un-hittable AC; and an assertion checked a never-set `prone` field against literal `false` rather than falsiness, when this engine's own convention (matching `isOffGuardFromConditions`'s `!!combatant.prone`) leaves an untouched condition field `undefined`, not defaulted to `false`.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Disarm/Reposition — not asked for. The real "at least one hand free" requirement on all four actions — this engine has no hand/equipment-state tracking for any combatant, the same documented class of gap as every other missing prerequisite mechanism in this project. Grabbed's own DC 5 flat-check-or-lose text for manipulate actions, and Restrained's "can't use manipulate actions" half — no trait data exists to know which of saveAttack/conditionSave/areaAttack/healAbility would even qualify. Immobilized's own "an external force must beat a DC to move you" clause — nothing in this roster's action set ever tries to forcibly move an already-immobilized creature. Any AI weight tuning specifically for choosing control actions over damage — the evaluator integration here is real and already measured to change `decide()`'s actual choice in the one scenario built to test it, but a broader tuning pass (how OFTEN should a real fight favor Grapple over a Strike) is exactly the kind of thing this project's own ablation-harness discipline (§17-§18) would want real data for, not a guess.

### Files

New: `reference/srd/player-core/condition-20-grabbed.json`, `condition-24-immobilized.json`, `condition-34-restrained.json`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/{conditions,legality,engine,decide}.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/evaluate/state-value.mjs`, `tools/character-builder/generate-raw-validation-fixtures.mjs` (all ten fixtures regenerated with real `athleticsModifier`). Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-legality.test.mjs`, `personnel-conditions.test.mjs`, `personnel-engine.test.mjs`, `personnel-decide.test.mjs` (all extended, no new files — matching how §22-§25's own condition/action additions extended these same four rather than forking new ones).

## 41. Stage 0 of the tactical-planning report — Stand/Crawl, and two live-verified regressions in §40's own work

Ingram, after §40 shipped Trip/Shove/Grapple: *"Now we need the logic for when and WHY these abilities would be used... start with stage 0."* Stage 0, per the tactical-planning report written in response to that same message: add Stand, restrict prone movement to Crawl/Stand, fix the evaluator's prone drift — the prerequisite fix before anything about Trip's own AI-usage logic can be measured honestly, since a tripped combatant currently strides away at full Speed, forever prone, and Trip's entire economic case is that the target has to spend a real action undoing it.

### The real bug Trip exposed

Prone existed before §40 (knockout has always caused it) but nothing had ever needed to CLEAR it outside the heal-a-downed-PC path, since an unconscious combatant is already out of the fight. Trip made it load-bearing for the first time, and `checkMoveLegality` had no prone gate at all — a tripped combatant simply Strode away unaffected.

### Real rules, mirrored before writing any code

`383-actions.json` (already locally mirrored, Player Core's own Basic Actions chapter, pg. 406-411) turned out to already carry Stand and Crawl verbatim — no fetch needed this time. **Stand** [one-action, Move]: "You stand up from being prone." No requirements, no roll. **Crawl** [one-action, Move]: requires being prone and a Speed of at least 10ft; "You move 5 feet by crawling and continue to stay prone." `condition-32-prone.json` (mirrored §40): "The only move actions you can use while prone are Crawl and Stand." `checkMoveLegality` gained a real prone gate plus an `allowWhileProne` parameter — Crawl's own wrapper (`checkCrawlLegal`) is the one caller that sets it, since Crawl is the rule's own second real exception, not a bypass of the rule it exists to satisfy. A real, intended consequence, not a special case: a combatant who is BOTH prone and immobilized (grabbed while already down) can't Stand either — Stand carries the move trait too, and `checkStandLegal` reflects that directly.

`decide.mjs` offers Stand unconditionally whenever prone. Crawl is deliberately NOT generated on its own — mirroring this file's own already-established precedent for Step (see that file's own header): with no Take Cover action and no difficult terrain yet, Crawl is dominated by Stand in every case this engine can currently produce. The action type and its legality/resolution are real and complete either way, for a hand-authored AI or a future extension to use directly.

### The evaluator drift, found while fixing the first one

`state-value.mjs`'s `bestExpectedDamage` computed an attacking combatant's own penalty by hand-rolling just the Frightened half (`- (attacker.frightened ?? 0)`) instead of calling `conditions.mjs`'s real `attackerConditionPenalty` — the exact "two statements of one rule" drift class `resolve.mjs`'s own header warns about. A prone attacker's real -2 was invisible to `threatExposure`/`offensiveReach` from the moment Trip shipped. Fixed by calling the real shared function directly; the doc comment above it now explains why both penalties belong there, not just one.

### Two more regressions, found re-verifying §40's own work against a live source

Researching Crawl's exact wording meant re-reading `383-actions.json` closely for the first time since it was mirrored — which turned up its own Escape entry, and it disagreed with what §40 built:

**Escape's real DC never involved Fortitude at all.** §40's implementation used "the Fortitude or Athletics DC of the creature grabbing you, whichever is higher," sourced from `skill-3-athletics.json`'s own embedded summary of Escape. `383-actions.json`'s real Basic Actions entry says something different — "typically the Athletics DC of a creature grabbing you," no Fortitude mentioned at all — and rather than trust one mirror over another, both were checked against the live, individually-fetched action page (`https://2e.aonsrd.com/actions/5-escape`), which matches `383-actions.json` exactly. Fixed: DC is `10 + the grappler's Athletics modifier`, full stop. (The real default check is your unarmed attack modifier, with Acrobatics or Athletics as explicit legal substitutes — this engine has no unarmed-attack-modifier concept distinct from a combatant's own named strikes, so the escaping creature's real, RAW-legal Athletics option is the one used, a documented choice among the rule's own alternatives, not a guess.) Proven with a regression test that gives the grappler an enormous Fortitude and a terrible Athletics score, asserting the DC reflects only the latter.

**Grapple's own critical-failure comment was describing the wrong alternative.** The real text (verified the same way, `https://2e.aonsrd.com/actions/64-grapple`) is "Your target can either grab you, as if it succeeded at using the Grapple action against you, or force you to fall and land prone" — not "push you up to 5 feet," which is what an earlier, less precise mirror had produced. The actual behavior (always resolving to the prone alternative, since this engine has no mechanism for a target's own reactive tactical choice) was already a defensible simplification either way; only the comment explaining what was being skipped was wrong. Fixed to describe the real alternative correctly.

Neither of these was asked for this pass — both were found as a direct side effect of doing the research properly for Stand/Crawl rather than assuming the existing Escape/Grapple work was already settled. Fixed in the same pass rather than left as known-wrong code sitting next to code being actively extended.

### Verification

`test/battle-simulator/personnel-legality.test.mjs`: the new prone gate on `checkMoveLegality`/`checkStepLegality` (blocked, and unaffected when not prone), `allowWhileProne` proven directly, `checkStandLegal`/`checkCrawlLegal` (positive/negative each, including the prone+immobilized interaction). `test/battle-simulator/personnel-engine.test.mjs`: Stand and Crawl resolve correctly via the sandbox (Crawl proven to stay prone, not accidentally clear it), a prone Stride is rejected in a real `runBattle`, standing then striding in the same turn restores real movement, and the Escape DC regression is pinned with an exact `dc` assertion. `test/battle-simulator/personnel-decide.test.mjs`: Stand is offered while prone and only while prone, Crawl is confirmed absent from generated proposals, and a real `decide()` end-to-end case proves a prone combatant with nothing worth attacking chooses to stand back up. `test/battle-simulator/personnel-state-value.test.mjs`: `bestExpectedDamage`'s prone penalty, and that it stacks correctly with Frightened. Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Everything past Stage 0 — Stage 1's tempo-denial evaluator generalization (`state.initiativeOrder` is still unread by `state-value.mjs`), Stage 2's whole-turn planning, and the group-play work, all deliberately left for their own passes, per the tactical-planning report's own staged structure and Ingram's own "start with stage 0." Crawl's own candidate generation, once Take Cover or difficult terrain exist to make it a real choice. Re-auditing every OTHER already-shipped mirror against its live source on spec — this pass fixed what it tripped over, not a systematic re-verification sweep.

### Files

Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/{legality,engine,decide}.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/evaluate/state-value.mjs`. Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-legality.test.mjs`, `personnel-engine.test.mjs`, `personnel-decide.test.mjs`, `personnel-state-value.test.mjs` (all extended, no new files).

## 42. Stage 1 of the tactical-planning report — generalized tempo denial, setup value, mobility denial, Hero Points, and morale/retreat

Ingram, after Stage 0 shipped: *"Youve mentioned the overall approach, now I want you to refine your AI logic ideas to specifically answer questions about how to create SF2E logic that answers the key parts of pathfinder/star finder combat optimization. Start with defining good effective combat AI... tuning for fights to be hard, seriously challenging even but not unwinnable... tune towards giving more of a challenge to a confident and competent group but also to pull punches whilst maintaining credibility when things go wrong for the players."* Then, once the refined report landed: *"continue to stage 1."* Stage 1, per that report's own staged plan, bundled five originally-separate threads into one pass: generalizing the old Stunned-only tempo pricing to every "owed action" state now modeled, pricing WHEN a debuff lands relative to initiative (setup value), pricing an immobilized enemy's frustrated mobility (the mirror image of the existing closing gradient), wiring in Hero Points (the one PC-only save-from-death resource this engine had never modeled), and a strictly opt-in morale/retreat behavior — the report's own answer to "pull punches while maintaining credibility" is never to touch dice, only to change what a losing side chooses to do.

### Generalized tempo denial (was stunDenial)

`state.initiativeOrder` existed on every state object since early in this engine's life but was confirmed unread by both `decide.mjs` and `state-value.mjs` before this pass (checked directly, twice). `tempoOwed()` replaces the old flat `stunnedValue` read with a real sum: Stunned's own already-tracked value (Player Core pg. 421, unchanged), Prone (a flat 1 — the real cost of Stand, per §41's own Basic Actions citation), and Grabbed/Restrained (the real expected number of Escape attempts before freedom, via `degreeProbabilities` against the exact grappler-Athletics-DC formula §41 already fixed — capped at 4 attempts so a near-impossible escape doesn't send the term toward infinity). Deliberately does NOT re-price Restrained's own "can't attack" half a second time — that's already priced via `bestExpectedDamage`'s restrained exclusion (§40) through threatExposure/offensiveReach, and double-counting the same fact through two mechanisms would silently inflate its true weight. The weight itself was renamed `tempoDenial` to match what it now actually prices.

### Setup value

A debuff is worth more the sooner an ally can capitalize on it. `setupValue` reuses offensiveReach's own `bestExpectedDamage`/per-enemy-HP-cap machinery exactly, restricted to allies listed after `actingCombatantId` in `state.initiativeOrder` — i.e. who haven't acted yet this round and can genuinely follow up. `evaluateState` gained a fourth, optional `actingCombatantId` parameter (default `null`) specifically so every existing 2/3-argument call site — every test, every pre-Stage-1 caller in `decide.mjs` — produces an identical score to before; only this new term ever reads it. Additive on top of offensiveReach, not a replacement: an already-acted ally's potential is still real, just not worth as much as an about-to-act ally's immediate one.

### Mobility denial

The mirror image of the existing closing gradient: closing only ever prices MY side's need to approach, so an enemy pinned by Grapple/Immobilized out of its own useful range was previously invisible to the evaluator — grappling a melee attacker already out of reach scored no differently than grappling one already adjacent. `mobilityDenial` scores that frustration directly, same unit as closing (squares short of range), same starting magnitude.

### Hero Points

Player Core pg. 405, previously unmirrored into any mechanic despite being in the SRD mirror since the original 205-article pull: 1 Hero Point at session start, spend-all-you-have (minimum 1) to avoid death outright, no gain or increase to Wounded from THIS path specifically ("if you already had that condition, you don't lose it or decrease its value" — the exact asymmetry vs. a natural recovery-check stabilization, which always grants Wounded). `trySpendHeroPointToAvoidDeath` intercepts before applying at exactly the three real points a combatant's dying value could reach 4 — `applyKnockout`, `worsenDyingFromDamage`, `resolveRecoveryCheck` — spending the resource, forcing `dyingValue` to 0 and `currentHP` to 0 (stabilized, not healed), and in `resolveRecoveryCheck`'s case specifically routing around the wounded-increment line entirely rather than granting it and then trying to undo it. PC-only, matching the chapter's own scope: `heroPoints: 1` was added to all five real PC fixture builders in `generate-raw-validation-fixtures.mjs`; the five NPC builders were deliberately left untouched.

### Morale / retreat

GM Core §791 pg. 78, "Morale": *"While PCs occasionally encounter truly fanatical zealots or single-minded creatures that would never back down from a fight, most creatures — even non-sapient creatures like animals — back down from a battle they're obviously losing."* `checkShouldFlee` fires as a DECISIVE override in `decide()` — ahead of the normal evaluator race entirely, not one more candidate competing in it — because §31's own audit already found a losing "disengage" candidate routinely loses that exact competition to a mediocre attack even at 1 HP, precisely the failure mode a real fleeing creature wouldn't exhibit. No numeric threshold exists in the mirrored rules; the two conditions checked are a stated judgment call, not a guess dressed up as one: personally bloodied (own HP at or below a third) AND this side clearly worse off than the opposition (mean living-HP fraction strictly lower). `me.temperament` recognizes "fanatical" and "single-minded" as GM Core's own two named exceptions, verbatim; a third value, "mindless", was added as this project's own reasonable extension beyond the quoted text (a Mindless creature has no self-preservation concept to invoke in the first place) — flagged in the code as an addition, not a citation. Strictly opt-in via `state.moraleEnabled` (default falsy) — every existing scenario's behavior is completely unaffected unless a caller turns it on — matching the report's own two-mode argument directly: Validation mode (the honest, unmodified baseline used to check an encounter's XP budget against what GM Core says it means) must never carry table-assist behavior like this; only Table-Assist mode should.

### Bugs found and fixed

**Real, in the mechanic, caught by hand before ever shipping**: an early draft of `mobilityDenial` gated on `enemy.currentHP <= 0` directly rather than capping the credit at the enemy's own remaining HP — the exact discontinuous-cliff bug §17 (offensiveReach) and §32 (closing) already found and fixed elsewhere. A far-pinned immobilized enemy at 1 HP worth real, possibly large credit would have had that credit vanish in one step the instant a strike killed them, scoring the killing blow WORSE than leaving them alive. Fixed to cap via `Math.min(denied, enemy.currentHP)`, mirroring offensiveReach/setupValue exactly; proven with a dedicated regression test sweeping the full HP range down to 0, not just the last step.

**A real citation drift, caught mid-verification of this very section**: `checkShouldFlee`'s own doc comment quoted GM Core's Morale passage as *"Except for mindless creatures or single-minded creatures..."* — it actually reads *"truly fanatical zealots or single-minded creatures."* "Mindless" is a real, distinct PF2e/SF2E trait (no intelligence, immune to mental effects); conflating it with the quoted "fanatical zealot" exception was an invented substitution, not a paraphrase. Fixed to quote verbatim, with "mindless" kept as a third exception value but now honestly labeled as this project's own extension rather than attributed to the rulebook — and a new test added specifically for "fanatical" (GM Core's own actually-quoted first exception, which had zero coverage until this fix, since the original two tests only covered "mindless" and "single-minded").

**Two test-authoring bugs, not in the mechanic** (same honest distinction §25 and §40 already drew for their own): the first `tempoDenial`-generalization test compared a stunned enemy against a merely-`prone` one using `makeState()`'s default ranged-armed ally, not accounting for the fact that Prone ALSO grants a real, already-existing (§40) off-guard AC credit against incoming attacks — so the diff picked up both effects at once (measured: +1.352 instead of the intended isolated +1.000) and the test's equality assertion failed against genuinely correct evaluator behavior. Fixed by isolating both prone/stunned tests to strikeless combatants on both sides, so offensiveReach contributes a flat zero regardless of prone and only tempoOwed's own formula is exercised. Separately, a `mobilityDenial` "already within reach" test asserted `pinned === free` when toggling only `grappledBy`, not accounting for the fact that the SAME toggle also feeds tempoOwed's own new grapple-escape-cost pricing (unconditional on distance) — fixed by isolating mobilityDenial's contribution from tempoOwed's known, computable contribution via the identical "subtract the known term" technique the adjacent NON-immobilized test already used. Both were caught by the required full test-file run this pass, not by inspection — direct evidence for why every new evaluator term ships with the test run actually executed, not just written.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Stage 2 (whole-turn planning — the report's own next step, letting a combatant weigh a full 3-action sequence rather than one action at a time), Stage 3 (group-level coordinated plays / blackboard architecture), and Stage 4 (adversarial search against a modeled enemy response) — all deliberately left for their own passes, matching Stage 0's own precedent of stopping at exactly what was asked. The Reactive Strike gap the report's own Part 2 flagged as its single biggest remaining item (no creature in this roster has ever been modeled taking an out-of-turn reaction) — untouched. Any real weight-tuning/ablation pass for `setupValue`/`mobilityDenial`/`lowHpCaution`-style caution against morale specifically, or a numeric threshold for `checkShouldFlee` grounded in measured data rather than judgment — all five new/changed weights are flagged NOT YET MEASURED in the same disciplined style §22/§35 already established, real data to correct them deliberately not invented here. A `temperament` field in any of the data/schema JSON Schema files — it exists only as an informal, optional field on the simulator's internal combatant shape for now, the same status `aggression` (§22) already has.

### Files

Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/{decide,conditions,engine}.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/evaluate/state-value.mjs`, `tools/character-builder/generate-raw-validation-fixtures.mjs` (`heroPoints: 1` on all five PC builders). Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-state-value.test.mjs`, `personnel-conditions.test.mjs`, `personnel-decide.test.mjs` (all extended, no new files). Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward: 935/935 passing.

## 43. Stage 2 of the tactical-planning report — whole-turn planning, a real MODE-vs-MEAN bug in Trip/Shove, and a real performance crisis

Ingram: *"continue to stage 2."* Stage 2, per the report's own staged plan, is "plan a whole turn, not one action at a time" — its own motivating case: *"Trip → Strike → Strike is far stronger than any of its parts scored alone, and greedy selection will rarely find it, because Trip's immediate delta is small."* The report flagged the branching-factor risk directly (*"candidates cubed is not affordable, and there is already a documented performance cliff here"* — design doc §22 Bug 3) and offered two standard mitigations: beam search over partial plans, or a small set of named turn templates that the search fills in. This pass built the template approach — and then, separately and much more expensively, had to earn the report's own warning about cost the hard way.

### The mechanism: five turn templates, filled by the existing per-action scoring machinery

`decide()` no longer scores one action at a time at full competence. `planTurn` (`decide.mjs`) runs a small portfolio — `control-then-attack`, `double-strike`, `reposition-and-shoot`, `disengage-and-heal` (the report's own four names, verbatim) plus `unconstrained` (this project's own fifth addition, a safety net matching every proposal type at every phase — since `planTurn` always takes the max score across the whole portfolio, its presence is what guarantees the search can never settle on a worse plan than the pre-Stage-2 baseline would have played). Each template is an ordered list of per-action TYPE FILTERS; the last filter repeats for any remaining actions once the list runs out, so a 2-phase template like `control-then-attack` still fills a real 3-action turn (Trip, Strike, Strike — not Trip, Strike, hold). Every phase's candidate is picked by the exact same after-minus-before scoring `decide()` has always used for a single action, reused per phase; a phase whose own filter matches nothing legal (e.g. `control-then-attack` on a combatant with no Athletics at all) gracefully falls back to the full unfiltered pool rather than being thrown out — a template is a bias toward a SHAPE of play, never a reason to take a worse action than the best one genuinely available. `decide()` returns the winning plan's FIRST action only (`generateProposals`'s existing contract with the turn loop is unchanged), with the plan's own template name and full step sequence folded into `reason` — the report's own "readable rationales for free" benefit of this approach over a raw search.

`competence < 1` is deliberately untouched — it stays on the exact pre-Stage-2 single-action greedy-then-softmax path. Competence already models an imperfect actor sampling among single-action choices; stacking multi-action lookahead on top of that is a real, separate design question this pass doesn't invent an answer to, and a skilled tactician planning several actions ahead vs. a middling one reacting one action at a time is also the more natural reading of what "competence" already meant in this file's own header.

### Two flagship proof cases, both hand-verified against the real evaluator before being written into test assertions

**Grapple → Strike → Strike** (`control-then-attack`): a combatant with real Athletics facing an AC-18 enemy. A pure single-action-isolated comparison shows Strike (6.8), Grapple (4.3), Trip (2.2) — greedy would press the attack immediately. `planTurn` instead finds Grapple → Strike → Strike scores 17.5 total, a full 42% better than three plain Strikes (12.3) — because Grabbed's own real off-guard bonus (Player Core) makes the two follow-up Strikes meaningfully more likely to land, a payoff completely invisible to a chooser that only ever asks "what does THIS action alone look like."

**Reposition-and-shoot, a second, distinct case**: a ranged attacker (Rifle, works at any range) facing a devastating melee-only threat (4d6+10) standing adjacent. A lone retreat scores far below a lone Strike in isolation — greedy would never retreat here. `planTurn` finds retreat-then-two-shots scores 12.8, beating three shots taken from melee range (10.2) — trading away only the worst, heavily MAP-penalized third shot for real safety from the melee threat's own devastating return swing next round (measured: ~4.3 points of pure positional/threatExposure benefit from distance alone). This ALSO exposed a real confound in a pre-existing §22 test (`lowHpCaution END TO END: at full HP... presses the attack`): its own scenario armed the combatant with a ranged weapon specifically to isolate lowHpCaution's HP-scaled urgency from any other reason to retreat — but Stage 2 now correctly discovers this SEPARATE, valid reason to retreat even at full HP. Fixed by giving that pair's shared fixture a melee-only weapon instead (so retreating genuinely costs all its own offense again, restoring the original isolation), and adding the ranged-kiting case above as its own dedicated positive test.

**A third, unplanned confirmation, found by a pre-existing test firing rather than a hand-built scenario**: `personnel-lunar17-vs-security-goon.test.mjs` carried an honest, deliberately-pinned §19 finding — *"Demoralize is never actually chosen in this specific 1v1 matchup under current weights... if this ever becomes nonzero, that's real news... update this test's assertion (and its comment) to match, don't just delete it."* It's now nonzero: 5 real Demoralize attempts across the same 10-seed sweep that used to show zero. Same mechanism as the two hand-built cases — Demoralize's own real Frightened application (Player Core) makes a follow-up attack meaningfully likelier to land, invisible to a chooser scoring it alone, real once `control-then-attack` can see the whole sequence — but discovered in the wild, by an existing regression test doing exactly the job its own comment described it for, rather than by a scenario built to demonstrate the point. Fixed per that comment's own explicit instruction: the assertion now proves the qualitative finding (sometimes chosen, not dominant — 5 attempts across 10 full battles, several seeds still never find it worthwhile) rather than re-pinning an exact count that was never the load-bearing part of the original claim.

### A real bug in Trip/Shove, found chasing an unrelated anomaly

Early testing found the search preferring "Grapple → Trip → Trip" (repeatedly re-tripping an already-prone target) over ever attacking at all. The cause: `EXPECTED_OUTCOMES.trip`/`shove` (added in §40/§41) computed the MODE (whichever degree — critical success, success, etc. — is merely most likely) and then applied ITS full outcome as if certain, rather than a true probability-weighted expectation — exactly the MODE-vs-MEAN conflation `expectedStrikeDamage` (`resolve.mjs`) has always correctly avoided by weighting success and critical success separately. A single pre-Stage-2 `decide()` call never had reason to attempt the SAME control action against the SAME target twice in a row (each real call re-evaluates fresh against the real post-action state), so this was latent; Stage 2's own multi-action lookahead was the first thing to actually chain repeated Trips within one search and expose it as a real, if narrow, free-damage source. Fixed: `appliedDamage`/`pushFeet` are now `probs.criticalSuccess`-weighted true expectations, not mode-gated all-or-nothing values. Two existing exact-value Shove tests needed their own numbers corrected (10 → 9.75, `makeGrappler`/`makeHeld`'s own modifier/DC combo turns out to be 19/20 critical success, not a literal 100%, once a natural 1 always downgrading is accounted for) — the OLD number was itself an artifact of the bug, not a deliberate design choice. A close (not overwhelming) matchup test and a repeated-Trip-no-longer-double-dips test both pin the fix directly.

### The performance crisis — measured, not guessed, matching this project's own §22 Bug 3 precedent

The first working version of `planTurn` (one fresh sandbox per template, all phases walked independently) was never load-tested before a real multi-seed run was attempted — a real process ran for over an hour and never completed, discovered only because Ingram had to ask directly. Profiled properly afterward, in three stages, each measured before moving to the next:

1. **A single `decide()` call, real 8-combatant walled map**: pre-Stage-2 baseline ~0.68s; naive Stage 2 ~2.66s (3.9x). Root cause: every template pays for `generateProposals` (real Dijkstra pathfinding) AND its own full candidate scoring independently, even though every template starts from the identical (state, turn) at phase 0.
2. **Fix 1 — share phase 0's scoring across all five templates** (they all start identical, by construction): ~2.39s. A real but modest improvement — most of the cost turned out to live in phases 1-2, where templates have genuinely diverged.
3. **Fix 2 — generalize to a full memoized search**, keyed on the exact SEQUENCE of proposals applied so far (a stable string key): two templates that reach the identical sequence are PROVABLY in the identical state (`EXPECTED_OUTCOMES` has no rng, the same determinism guarantee `forkTurnSandbox`'s own header already documents), so reusing one's scored candidates for the other is exact, not approximate. `reposition-and-shoot` and `disengage-and-heal` share an identical phase-0 filter and so ALWAYS collapse into one branch this way, not just when they happen to coincide. Measured: ~1.08s on the same synthetic 2-combatant scenario used for stages 1-2 — but on the REAL 8-combatant RANDOM_ARCS map, where templates diverge far more (real distinct enemies to filter on), this alone only brought a blind-exploration round-1 sample from ~21.5s down to ~14.8s across 8 combatants — nowhere near enough.
4. **Fix 3 — skip movement-candidate generation for phases whose filter could never select "move" anyway**: `isControlType`/`isAttackType`/`isHealType` phases were paying for the SAME expensive `bestDestinationToward`/`bestDestinationAway`/`bestCoverDestination` pathfinding as every other phase, then filtering every one of those candidates straight back out. `generateProposals` gained an `includeMovement` parameter (default true, every pre-Stage-2 caller unaffected); `planTurn`'s cache key now also carries this flag, since a "with movement" and "without movement" candidate set at the same sequence are genuinely different results. Combined with fix 2's shared pathfinding cache (`generateProposals` also gained an optional `externalDestCache` parameter, threaded through the whole `planTurn` call — the one deliberately accepted imprecision: a Shove earlier in the same plan can move a DIFFERENT combatant in a way this cache's key doesn't capture, but `attemptAction`'s own legality check remains the sole authority, so a stale-optimistic candidate is simply rejected like any other illegal proposal, never silently accepted): the same round-1, 8-combatant sample dropped to ~7.8s — a real single battle (7 rounds, `party-defeat`) now completes in 36.8s end to end, down from a run that never completed in over an hour.

This is NOT back to the ~0.68s pre-Stage-2 baseline, and isn't pretending to be — whole-turn planning genuinely costs more than single-action greedy, and this project's own testing culture would rather ship an honest, working, moderately-slower AI than force a number that looks better than it is. A dedicated performance regression test (`personnel-decide.test.mjs`, generous — 10x the fix's own measured cost) pins this so the exact shape of this bug can never silently return.

**A second, real cost this pass forced**: several pre-existing multi-seed battle tests (`personnel-complex-battle.test.mjs`'s 4v4 RANDOM_ARCS pair, previously 15 and 20 seeds at a 60-round cap) took the better part of an hour between them once Stage 2's real cost applied — cut to 6/8 seeds at a 30/40-round cap. Neither test's own PURPOSE needed the original counts: an illegal-action regression or a genuine mix of outcomes in a fair fight shows up just as reliably in single-digit seeds as in 15-20. The new `partyManeuvers` end-to-end test (below) is scoped the same way from the start — 3 seeds, a 20-round cap — for the identical reason.

### Proving the report's own two explicit verification asks

**"A no-maneuvers baseline... if the maneuver-capable AI does not measurably win more, the maneuvers are decoration."** `personnel-complex-battle.mjs`'s `buildScenario` gained `partyManeuvers` (default true) — false strips `athleticsModifier` from every PARTY fixture after loading, which `generateProposals`'s own existing gate means disables EXACTLY Trip/Shove/Grapple for the party (Escape stays legal regardless — Player Core's own "everyone can always try" carve-out) and leaves the opposition's own capability untouched. A new registered scenario, `complex-4v4-no-maneuvers-random-arcs`, is IDENTICAL to `complex-4v4-random-arcs` in every other respect. `analysis/maneuver-baseline-comparison.mjs` (mirrors `xp-budget-battery.mjs`'s own `runScenarioSweep`/`aggregateSweep` directly, reused rather than re-derived) runs both head-to-head across the same seeds and reports win rate, final HP, damage taken, and rounds side by side, appending to the same accumulating `battle-history.md` design doc §39 already established.

**"A conversion rate... whether an ally actually cashed in on the setup before it expired."** `tactical-metrics.mjs` gained `maneuverConversionStats` — for every successful Trip/Grapple, scans forward through the battle's real chronological action order for the first of: a real attack-roll action against the same target FROM THE MANEUVERING SIDE (converted), the target's own condition-clearing action (Stand for Trip, a successful Escape for Grapple — closes the window unconverted), Grapple's own real, already-logged `expiresEndOfRound` timer passing (natural expiry, unconverted), or the end of the log (never converted). Both stopping conditions are read straight off data the engine already logs, not approximated — exact within the battle it measures. Shove is deliberately excluded: it has no persistent condition and no "am I still benefiting from this" question a later attack can answer the way Trip/Grapple's real off-guard bonus can.

### HONEST FINDING: the real comparison run, and it does NOT say what the report's own hypothesis expected

The report's own framing treated conversion rate as the interesting question and win rate as almost a formality — high conversion would mean the maneuvers are "for a reason." Run for real (15 seeds, same roster, same seeds, `battle-history.md` carries the full dated entry):

| | WITH maneuvers | WITHOUT maneuvers |
|---|---|---|
| Party win rate | 40.0% | **66.7%** |
| Mean party final HP | 21.5% | **49.4%** |
| Mean party damage taken | 112.6 | 100.9 |
| Mean rounds to resolution | 7.9 | 9.3 |

Conversion is excellent — Trip 35 attempts/96.4% converted, Grapple 103 attempts/97.0% converted — this is NOT the "hollow maneuver use" `maneuverConversionStats` was built to catch. All four metrics point the same direction anyway: the maneuver-capable party wins less often, ends fights hurt roughly twice as badly, takes more damage, and resolves fights faster (consistent with losing faster, not winning faster). This is the OPPOSITE of the report's own conditional ("if the maneuver-capable AI does not measurably win more, the maneuvers are decoration") — it's not decoration, it's a measurable net cost, despite firing exactly as designed every time.

Not chased further this pass — diagnosing WHY is real, separate work (candidates worth naming honestly rather than guessing at silently: `tempoDenial`/`mobilityDenial`/`setupValue`'s own weights, all still flagged NOT YET MEASURED since the moment they were added in §42, may simply overvalue control relative to `enemyDamage` in a way that scores well on the internal heuristic without translating to real wins; ~103 Grapple attempts across 15 battles is a lot of actions not spent attacking, and Grapple specifically requires melee proximity that a real attack wouldn't). Flagged here exactly as this project's own convention demands — a real, measured, surprising result, not quietly re-tuned around or hidden. Whether this is a weight-calibration problem or something the templates themselves are structurally causing is the natural next real question, not decided here.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Stage 3 (group-level coordinated plays / blackboard architecture) and Stage 4 (adversarial search) — both explicitly held pending this stage's own results, per the report's own recommendation ("do Stage 0, then Stage 1, then Stage 2 — and stop there to reassess"). The Reactive Strike gap the report's own Part 2 flagged as its single biggest remaining item — still untouched. Caching or persisting a whole-turn PLAN across the 2-3 real `decide()` calls within one turn — deliberately rejected, not merely deferred: each call re-plans fresh against the REAL post-action state (not the EXPECTED-VALUE sandbox's own assumption), so a Trip that actually fails for real correctly triggers a fresh, honest re-evaluation next call rather than blindly continuing a plan built on an assumption that didn't hold. The win-rate regression just measured was diagnosed and fixed in the very next pass — see §44.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/maneuver-baseline-comparison.mjs`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/{decide,engine}.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/tactical-metrics.mjs` (`maneuverConversionStats`), `tools/battle-simulator/scenarios/personnel-complex-battle.mjs` (`partyManeuvers`), `tools/battle-simulator/run-personnel-scenario.mjs` (`complex-4v4-no-maneuvers-random-arcs`). Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-decide.test.mjs`, `personnel-engine.test.mjs`, `personnel-complex-battle.test.mjs` (all extended, seed counts on its two heaviest pre-existing 4v4 tests reduced — see this section's own performance writeup), `tactical-benchmark.test.mjs` (extended, `maneuverConversionStats` coverage), `personnel-lunar17-vs-security-goon.test.mjs` (its own Demoralize tripwire updated per its own prior instruction), `run-personnel-scenario-cli.test.mjs` (the real-registry name list gained the new scenario); `maneuver-baseline-comparison.test.mjs` (new). Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward: 957/957 passing, ~37.6 minutes — up from this project's usual ~25 (see this section's own performance writeup for why, and what was already done to keep it from being far worse).

## 44. Diagnosing and fixing §43's own win-rate regression — reading real battle logs, and splitting tempoDenial

Ingram: *"dig into the win-rate regression - if you paste the actual log of a characters moves I bet they are spamming the wrong move too often and giving up perfectly good chances to deal damage. Find out what is happening, I bet the logs will explain everything."* Then, once the mechanism was found: *"propose a fix and test it."*

### The logs already had the answer

A dumper script (scratch, not committed — the real per-action log already carries everything needed: type, target, real roll degree, `targetHPAfter`, and `decisionReason` with the AI's own full planned sequence) run on the SAME seed under both conditions made the mechanism visible directly, no instrumentation needed. `log-seed1-with.txt`, round 4: Vorza Strikes Xenowarden once, then spends her remaining two actions Tripping and Grappling a DIFFERENT enemy (Erabryth) — dealing zero damage to either target with those two actions. `log-seed1-without.txt`, the identical seed and round: the same two actions are two real Strikes that take Xenowarden from 20 HP to 4 — nearly a kill. Multiplied across a whole party, every turn, this is exactly Ingram's own prediction: real damage opportunities given up for moves that don't pay for themselves.

### The mechanism, confirmed against real fixtures

Trip/Shove/Grapple are Athletics checks, never attack rolls (Player Core pg. 193, §40's own citation) — so unlike a Strike, they never take Multiple Attack Penalty. A real Strike's own expected value shrinks hard on a turn's 2nd/3rd action (0/-5/-10); `tempoDenial`'s credit for applying Prone or Grabbed does not shrink at all. Probed directly against the real Vorza-Moonshot/Erabryth fixtures at the exact HP/position from the log, 3rd action of the turn:

| Action (3rd action of the turn, real fixtures) | Isolated evaluator score |
|---|---|
| Strike (Vorza's Zero Pistol, -10 MAP) | 1.225 |
| Trip | 1.477 (+21%) |
| Grapple | 1.958 (**+60%**) |

A fresh Grapple — dealing zero damage — outscored continuing to attack. The winning template for that exact turn was `unconstrained`, the one that reproduces old pre-Stage-2 greedy behavior exactly, confirming this was never a Stage-2-specific bug: it's a `tempoDenial` pricing question from §42 (Stage 1) that the whole-turn search didn't create, only exposed, by being the first thing that made a no-maneuvers comparison possible at all.

### The fix: `tempoDenial` no longer prices Prone/Grabbed at Stunned's own rate

§42 priced Stunned (Player Core pg. 421 — a CERTAIN, immediate, already-resolved loss, no further roll involved) and Prone/Grabbed's own "owed actions" (CONTESTED by a real future roll the target might simply win — Stand is a free action available any time; Escape can just succeed) at the identical `tempoDenial` rate. `tempoOwed` is now Stunned-only; a new function, `maneuverTempoOwed`, prices Prone (flat 1) and Grabbed/Restrained (the same real expected-escape-attempts math as before, still capped at 4) at a new, separate weight, `maneuverTempoDenial: 0.3` — deliberately reusing `setupValue`'s own existing rate rather than inventing a new number, since both represent the same real category: value that depends on a future event, not Stunned's own settled certainty. Re-probed the identical Vorza/Erabryth position: Strike 1.225, Trip 0.777, Grapple 0.685 — both maneuvers now correctly score below continuing to attack.

### Verification: the flagship synergy case still holds, and the real comparison reverses

The §43 flagship test (Grapple → Strike → Strike beating three plain Strikes by 42%) still passes unmodified — genuine synergy, where the follow-up strikes actually land, is driven by the off-guard AC credit already priced through `offensiveReach`/`bestExpectedDamage`, not by `tempoOwed`'s own flat credit, so lowering that credit doesn't touch it. What it does remove is control applied WITHOUT a real follow-up. The full `battle-simulator` suite (957 tests, five of them updated for the new split — the old assertions assumed Prone/Grapple priced at Stunned's own rate, which is now precisely the bug) re-runs clean.

The real test is the same 15-seed no-maneuvers comparison from §43, re-run after the fix:

| | WITH maneuvers (before fix) | WITH maneuvers (after fix) | WITHOUT maneuvers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Party win rate | 40.0% | **60.0%** | 46.7% |
| Mean party final HP | 21.5% | **46.0%** | 31.9% |
| Mean party damage taken | 112.6 | **94.5** | 104.4 |

The regression doesn't just shrink — it reverses. The maneuver-capable party now measurably outperforms the no-maneuvers baseline on every metric, which is what the report's own original conditional predicted all along ("if the maneuver-capable AI does not measurably win more, the maneuvers are decoration") — it took a wrong weight, not a wrong mechanism, to make that conditional read the other way. A secondary, unplanned confirmation: Trip usage went UP (35 → 67 attempts, 96.4% → 100% converted) while Grapple dropped sharply (103 → 8 attempts, still 91.7% converted) — with Grapple's inflated ceiling gone, the AI reaches for it far more sparingly, and Trip's own cheaper, more modest credit gets used more purposefully instead of being crowded out.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

A broader ablation/re-measurement of `mobilityDenial`/`setupValue` against the same real comparison methodology — only `tempoDenial`'s own split was tested this pass, the one directly implicated by the log evidence. `0.3` for `maneuverTempoDenial` is a reasoned, tested-once starting point (reusing `setupValue`'s own established rate for the same category of conditional value), not a swept-and-optimized number — a real parameter search (proposed but not yet built — see the "Archetype Ladder" plan discussed 2026-08-30) could find a better value. The misleading template-label finding from §43 (a `disengage-and-heal` plan containing neither a move nor a heal, once its own phase-0 filter falls back to the full pool) — a real logging/readability gap, not fixed here.

### Files

Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/evaluate/state-value.mjs` (`tempoOwed`/`maneuverTempoOwed` split, `maneuverTempoDenial` weight). Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-state-value.test.mjs` (five tests updated for the new split, one new WEIGHTS relationship test). Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward: 958/958 passing, ~21.0 minutes — notably FASTER than §43's own post-Stage-2 baseline (~37.6 min), consistent with fights now resolving more decisively rather than churning on control that didn't pay off. Real comparison re-run: `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/battle-history.md` carries both the original regression and the post-fix reversal as two separate dated entries.

## 45. Real progress visibility for long-running simulations

Ingram, directly: *"it would be really nice if all simulations would output at the very least a percentage of progress or other information so that I can tell the difference between a broken test, a slow test and a test which is at, say, 15% progress."* Grounded in real, freshly-lived pain — §43/§44's own work needed several multi-minute sweeps, more than one of which had to be diagnosed by killing the process and checking whether `node` was still alive, because nothing printed until the very end.

Same message also recorded a standing project-level goal, saved to memory rather than design doc (it shapes HOW future AI work gets judged, not a specific mechanism): the battle-simulator AI's target is **maximum tactical strength first** — PF2e/SF2E's own math assumes a GM playing NPCs close to optimally, so a maximally strong AI is the accurate simulation of a competent GM, not a softened one. Difficulty tuning for the real table is a later, separate, deliberate step (the existing `competence` knob, morale, and the proposed archetype system); low-intelligence creatures (mindless, animal, fanatic) get there through an explicit low-intelligence profile, never through a generally weaker AI. See `feedback_ai_strength_target` in memory for the full statement — recorded there, not duplicated here, since CLAUDE.md's own convention keeps standing behavioral guidance in memory and this design doc to mechanisms actually built.

### Two real hooks, both optional, zero cost to any existing caller

`runBattle(scenario)` (`personnel-engine/engine.mjs`) calls `scenario.onRoundStart?.(round, roundLimit)` once at the top of every round. An absent field is simply `undefined` — every existing call site across the whole test suite is unaffected, proven directly (a dedicated test asserts the omitted-callback path stays silent and unaffected, alongside every other test in the suite that already omits it).

`runScenarioSweep(scenarioName, seedSpec, { onProgress })` (`analysis/xp-budget-battery.mjs`) wires that hook through for a whole seed sweep, emitting two real, honestly-shaped event types to whatever renderer the caller provides: `{type: "round", seedIndex, seedTotal, seed, round, roundLimit}` — throttled to round 1 and every 5th round after, so a single long battle shows real forward motion without flooding the console — and `{type: "seedComplete", seedIndex, seedTotal, seed, outcome, rounds, elapsedMs}`, the real result of that seed, not a guess. `analysis/maneuver-baseline-comparison.mjs`'s own `runComparison` passes the same option straight through to both of its own sweeps (WITH and WITHOUT maneuvers).

`logProgress` is the one real default renderer — one terse line per event — wired into both scripts' own CLI `main()` functions. Library and test callers get complete silence unless they explicitly opt in, so `npm test`'s own output is unaffected; a real, live 2-seed run shows exactly the shape asked for:

```
--- WITH maneuvers (complex-4v4-random-arcs) ---
[1/2] seed 1: round 1/60...
[1/2] seed 1: round 5/60...
[1/2] seed 1: party-victory in 8 rounds (30890ms)
[2/2] seed 2: round 1/60...
...
```

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Progress output for `node --test` itself — Node's own test runner buffers stdout when redirected to a file rather than a TTY (confirmed directly this session: output that looked frozen for many minutes turned out to just be sitting in an unflushed buffer, not evidence of a hang), and that buffering is Node's own behavior, not something this project's code controls. The extra `console.log` traffic these hooks add happens to flush the same buffer more often as a side effect, but that is incidental, not a fix for the underlying mechanism. A real, general "percentage" figure (rather than "round N of limit M," which is already the real, honest unit — most battles resolve well before hitting `roundLimit`, so a literal percentage-of-roundLimit would usually UNDERSTATE progress, not report it accurately) — round/roundLimit was kept as the more honest number rather than inventing a smoother-looking one. Wiring `onRoundStart` into `runBattle`'s OWN direct callers outside the sweep scripts (e.g. a bare `node -e` one-liner) — available to any caller now, just not defaulted on anywhere else yet.

### Files

Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/engine.mjs` (`onRoundStart` hook), `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/xp-budget-battery.mjs` (`onProgress` param + `logProgress`, wired into `runBattery`), `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/maneuver-baseline-comparison.mjs` (`onProgress` passthrough, wired into `main`). Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-engine.test.mjs`, `xp-budget-battery.test.mjs`, `maneuver-baseline-comparison.test.mjs` (all extended, no new files). Memory: `feedback_ai_strength_target` (new). Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward: 963/963 passing, ~21.4 minutes.

## 46. The Archetype Ladder, Stage A — a real, nameable AI profile object

Ingram: *"Start Stage A of the archetype ladder - then test it - then report on what's been built and what still remains to be built."* Stage A, per the plan proposed 2026-08-30: *"The profile object and its plumbing. Named profiles, per-combatant assignment, plus the two guard tests (completeness, golden-log). Absorbs `partyManeuvers`. Small · blocks everything else · start here."*

### The problem this fixes

Before this pass, "an AI" wasn't a real, nameable thing — it was a function (`decide`), a module-level constant (`DEFAULT_WEIGHTS`), and behavior scattered across per-combatant fixture fields (`aggression`, `temperament`), a per-state flag (`moraleEnabled`), and a per-call argument (`competence`). Nothing about that could be frozen, diffed, or handed to a specific character. `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/ai-profiles.mjs` makes a profile a plain, frozen object that fully determines how a combatant plays.

### Two rules, both load-bearing

**Complete, never merged.** `assertCompleteWeights(weights)` throws immediately if a profile's own `weights` doesn't have EXACTLY `DEFAULT_WEIGHTS`' key set — not a subset (silent `NaN` risk, since `evaluateState` multiplies weight values straight into the score and a missing key computes `undefined * n = NaN`) and not a superset (a stale or typo'd key nobody's reading). Called once, directly, at import time on `BASELINE_V1`'s own weights — a real, immediate self-check, not a hope.

**Frozen means pinned numbers, not a live reference.** `BASELINE_V1.weights` is a hand-copied SNAPSHOT of `DEFAULT_WEIGHTS`' real current values, verified directly against the live constant before being written down — NOT a reference to `DEFAULT_WEIGHTS` itself. If it referenced the live constant, the next time anyone tunes a weight (exactly what §44 just did to `tempoDenial`), the "frozen" baseline would silently move too, defeating the entire point of a checkpoint. `Object.freeze` (on the profile AND its own `weights` sub-object) turns accidental in-place mutation into a hard, immediate error.

### The real plumbing, all additive

`decide()` gained three new OPTIONAL options — `templates` (an array of turn-template NAMES, resolved against `decide.mjs`'s own real `TURN_TEMPLATES`), `moraleEnabled` (a per-call override; `checkShouldFlee` now takes `moraleEnabledOverride ?? state.moraleEnabled`, so two combatants in the SAME battle can differ — something the old state-level-only flag structurally couldn't express), and `disabledActionTypes` (a real, honest CAPABILITY restriction — `filterDisabledTypes` removes matching proposals before anything ever scores them, applied in both the whole-turn search and the `competence < 1` fallback path, not a preference that can be outweighed). All three default to their exact pre-§46 behavior when omitted — proven directly (personnel-decide.test.mjs's full 60 tests, all pre-existing, all still pass unmodified).

`personnel-complex-battle.mjs`'s `buildScenario` gained `partyProfile`/`oppositionProfile` — per-SIDE assignment (Stage A's own right-sized scope; true per-individual assignment is real future work, not needed yet). Deliberately **not** defaulted to `BASELINE_V1` even though the two currently produce numerically identical results: defaulting to a frozen snapshot would silently stop every existing scenario from tracking future evaluator tuning, inverting the exact "frozen in name only" risk this file's own header warns about. Omitted (every pre-§46 caller) means `decide()` is called with no options at all, byte-identical to before — proven directly, not assumed: a real battle run with profiles omitted and one run with `BASELINE_V1` explicitly assigned to both sides produce the EXACT same action sequence on the same seed.

### Absorbing `partyManeuvers`

`disabledActionTypes` is the general mechanism `partyManeuvers` always needed: `NO_MANEUVERS_V1` (`{...BASELINE_V1, disabledActionTypes: ["trip", "shove", "grapple"]}`) expresses the identical real intent — a combatant that can never attempt Trip/Shove/Grapple — as a decision-time filter rather than `partyManeuvers`' own fixture-data mutation (stripping `athleticsModifier`). Proven end to end through the real scenario builder: Security Goon assigned `NO_MANEUVERS_V1` never attempts a maneuver across several seeds, even though its own real fixture still carries its real `athleticsModifier` untouched — the restriction is genuinely at the decision layer, not a data hack. `partyManeuvers` itself is deliberately NOT replaced — it's already shipped, tested, and load-bearing for the real registered `complex-4v4-no-maneuvers-random-arcs` scenario (§43) — this proves the general mechanism works, without a risky rip-and-replace of already-verified code.

### The golden-sequence tests — proving "frozen" pins BEHAVIOR, not just numbers

A real, measured concern from the Ladder plan itself: an engine change (Stage 3's eventual blackboard, say) could make `BASELINE_V1` play differently tomorrow with byte-identical weights. `test/battle-simulator/ai-profiles.test.mjs` pins the EXACT real action sequence of a real battle (real Lunar-17/Security Goon fixtures, seed 42, a small fast arena) for `BASELINE_V1` — captured directly from a real `runBattle` call, not hand-derived — and a SECOND golden sequence with the opponent on `NO_MANEUVERS_V1` instead, which is not just longer but resolves to the OPPOSITE outcome (`party-victory` instead of `party-defeat`) on the identical seed — real, measured proof that a profile change is a genuine behavioral difference, not a label. Either sequence changing in the future means one of two real things happened: an intentional engine change (re-verify by hand, update the expected sequence, bump `ai-profiles.mjs`'s own `engineContract`), or a real regression this test exists to catch.

### Verification
