`test/battle-simulator/ai-profiles.test.mjs` (new, 14 tests): `assertCompleteWeights` (passes on a complete object, throws with the real missing/extra key names on an incomplete or over-complete one, reports both in one error), `BASELINE_V1`'s own weights matching live `DEFAULT_WEIGHTS` exactly, both profiles' frozen-ness, `applyProfileToCombatant`/`decideOptionsForProfile`'s real shapes, and the two golden sequences. `test/battle-simulator/personnel-complex-battle.test.mjs` (3 new tests, deliberately small/fast — 1v1, short `roundLimit` — since the golden sequences already carry the real proof): the omitted-vs-explicit-`BASELINE_V1` equivalence, `NO_MANEUVERS_V1` end to end through the real scenario builder, and `temperament`/`aggression` landing on deployed combatants. Every pre-existing test across the whole `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean, unmodified — the entire point of "additive, opt-in, zero cost to any existing caller."

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Stage B (mirror scenario + parallel tournament runner), Stage C (the archetype library itself — cautious/aggressive/flanker/mindless/zealot profiles), Stage D (automated weight search), Stage E (promotion into campaign data) — all deliberately held for their own passes, per the Ladder plan's own staged structure. True per-INDIVIDUAL profile assignment (right now, a whole side shares one profile) — side-level is Stage A's own right-sized scope; nothing currently needs finer grain. Migrating `partyManeuvers` itself onto the new mechanism, or removing the fixture-stripping code path — deliberately left alone, already correct, not broken. A profile-driven `aggression`/`temperament` value actually changing any CURRENTLY-shipped scenario's behavior — no real fixture sets either field today, so this pass's own plumbing has zero observable effect until Stage C's archetypes actually use it; `applyProfileToCombatant`'s own test suite proves the mechanism works, not that anything visibly changed yet.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/ai-profiles.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/ai-profiles.test.mjs`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/decide.mjs` (`templates`/`moraleEnabled`/`disabledActionTypes` options, `filterDisabledTypes`), `tools/battle-simulator/scenarios/personnel-complex-battle.mjs` (`partyProfile`/`oppositionProfile`). Tests: `test/battle-simulator/personnel-complex-battle.test.mjs` (3 new), `personnel-decide.test.mjs` (unmodified, all 60 re-verified passing as the real backward-compatibility proof). Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward: 980/980 passing, ~20.0 minutes.

## 47. The Archetype Ladder, Stage B — a real mirror match, and a real parallel tournament runner

Ingram: *"Complete Stage B."* Stage B, per the plan proposed 2026-08-30: *"Mirror scenario and the parallel tournament runner. A compact same-roster arena, worker-thread fan-out, a win-rate matrix appended to `battle-history.md`. Medium · unlocks affordable measurement."*

### The problem this fixes

"Is AI A stronger than AI B?" needs a mirror match — the IDENTICAL roster on both sides, only the AI profile differs — not `personnel-complex-battle.mjs`'s own asymmetric PC-vs-NPC roster, which conflates roster strength with AI strength (the exact confound §43/§44 hit directly: "PCs beat NPCs" says nothing about which AI is smarter once the rosters themselves also differ). And measuring that at any real sample size needs it to run fast — a single 8-combatant walled complex-battle run can take the better part of a minute, mostly real Dijkstra pathfinding, which makes even a 20-seed comparison slow going one seed at a time.

### `personnel-mirror.mjs` — a small, open, wall-free arena, on purpose

Deliberately not a reuse of `personnel-test-facility.mjs` (its `SETUPS` are fixed 1v1 attacker/defender narrative layouts, not generalized to N-combatant symmetric deployment) or the big complex-battle map (walled, RANDOM_ARCS blind-exploration opening — the expensive part of a slow battle is the EXPLORING, not the fighting, and a mirror match's whole point is measuring how well each side fights). `buildScenario({ seed, roster, partyProfile, oppositionProfile, roundLimit })` deploys `roster[i]` on the party (left edge) and an IDENTICAL mirrored copy on the opposition (right edge), same row, no walls, in a 30×20 arena sized so every real fixture's ranged weapons (50–120ft) reach across it without either side needing to blindly explore first.

`partyProfile`/`oppositionProfile` are REQUIRED here, unlike `personnel-complex-battle.mjs`'s own optional ones — a mirror match's entire purpose is comparing two named profiles, so silently falling back to `decide()`'s bare defaults on a missing one would defeat the point rather than being a safe no-op; it throws instead.

**A real, previously-undiscovered constraint this file exists to solve:** every real fixture file hard-codes its own `side` field (`lunar-17.json`'s own `"side": "party"`, `security-goon.json`'s own `"side": "opposition"`). Loading the SAME fixture file twice for a true mirror — one copy per side — would silently give BOTH copies the file's own baked-in `side` unless overridden, which would mean the engine's own faction logic (`splitByRelation`) treats them as allies and no real fight ever happens. `personnel-mirror.mjs` explicitly sets `side: "party"` / `side: "opposition"` on each deployed copy, regardless of what the fixture file itself says — proven directly in `personnel-mirror.test.mjs`: Security Goon's own PARTY-side mirror copy comes out `side: "party"` despite its file saying the opposite.

### `tournament-worker.mjs` / `tournament-runner.mjs` — a real, PERSISTENT worker pool, not one-shot spawns

Battles are pure and seed-deterministic (no shared state, no mid-battle I/O) — "embarrassingly parallel" per the Ladder artifact's own §04 cost-lever analysis — so `runTournamentParallel` fans a seed range out across real `worker_threads`. The first implementation spawned a fresh `new Worker()` per seed and measured a disappointing ~1.2–1.9x speedup; a direct measurement found why: spawning a worker costs ~80ms of real thread/module-startup time, comparable to a single mirror-match battle's own runtime, so most of the theoretical parallel gain was being spent on spawn overhead rather than real computation. The fix: `concurrency` workers are spawned ONCE and each pulls the next unclaimed seed off a shared cursor for its WHOLE lifetime (a persistent, pull-based pool — `tournament-worker.mjs` loops on `parentPort.on("message", ...)` rather than running once and exiting), amortizing startup cost across many battles instead of paying it per battle.

**A second real, measured finding, honestly reported rather than assumed:** on this project's own dev machine (16 logical / 8 physical AMD Ryzen 7 5800H cores), a 100-seed tournament at `concurrency = cpus().length - 1` (15) took 8251ms — but the SAME tournament at `concurrency = 4` took 6697ms, FASTER with fewer threads. This workload is real CPU-bound compute (dice rolls, state-value scoring, whole-turn template search), not I/O, so oversubscribing past the physical core count makes threads contend for the same execution units rather than adding throughput. `DEFAULT_CONCURRENCY` is now `Math.floor(cpus().length / 2)` — a more honest default than "every logical core" — with `concurrency` always overridable per call.

**The real, live speedup demonstration** (`node tools/battle-simulator/analysis/tournament-runner.mjs --roster=lunar-17.json,security-goon.json --party=baseline-v1 --opposition=no-maneuvers-v1 --seeds=1-50`, real numbers, appended to `battle-history.md`): 50 seeds at concurrency=8 in 4012ms vs. 8197ms run sequentially in-thread — a real 2.0x. `runTournamentSequential` isn't just a comparison baseline; it's what `runTournamentParallel`'s own test proves itself against directly — same seeds/profiles/roster, byte-identical real outcomes and round counts, since determinism means wall-clock time is the ONLY thing worker fan-out is allowed to change.

### The real win-rate matrix this unlocks

The same 50-seed run above is the first real measurement this mechanism produces, and it's a much starker, cleaner signal than §44's own asymmetric-roster comparison (60.0%/46.7%) precisely because a mirror match isolates the AI variable completely: `baseline-v1` (full maneuvers) wins 80.0% of mirror matches against `no-maneuvers-v1` (Trip/Shove/Grapple withheld) on the identical roster, with a 10.0% timeout rate and a mean 11.0 rounds to resolution. Appended as a dated section to `analysis/battle-history.md`, same accumulating-file convention as every other battery (§39).

**⚠ CORRECTION (§49):** this 80.0%/10.0% number, and every other real-win-rate number reported anywhere in this section, was measured through a real, previously-undiscovered confound in `personnel-mirror.mjs` itself — the "opposition" slot silently lost real PC-style dying/recovery survivability that the "party" slot kept, regardless of AI profile. §49 documents the fix and the corrected re-measurement; the DIRECTION of every finding in this section held up (maneuvers really do help), but every MAGNITUDE reported here is now known to have been inflated. Left unedited here deliberately, as a historical record of what was actually measured at the time — see §49 for the real numbers.

### Verification

`test/battle-simulator/personnel-mirror.test.mjs` (7 new tests): the two required-argument guards, real mirrored deployment (2x combatant count, correct per-copy `side` overriding the fixture's own baked-in value), symmetric positioning, the wall-free arena, and two real `runBattle` calls (one with a real profile asymmetry, one BASELINE_V1-vs-BASELINE_V1 true control) both resolving to real, legal outcomes with real damage exchanged between the two sides. `test/battle-simulator/tournament-runner.test.mjs` (8 new tests): `aggregateTournament`'s real win-rate/outcome-count math (plus its empty-input guard), the `PROFILES` registry, `runTournamentSequential`'s real per-seed output, and — the core Stage B correctness proof — `runTournamentParallel`'s real worker-thread results matching `runTournamentSequential`'s real in-thread results byte-for-byte (outcome and round count, per seed), plus progress-event coverage and a real concurrency-smaller-than-seed-count pool run. All kept to small seed counts/`roundLimit`s deliberately, since a real `Worker` spawn costs real wall-clock time and this suite should stay fast (~3.2s for the tournament-runner file). `ai-profiles.mjs` gained a small `PROFILES` registry (id → real frozen profile object) so a CLI (or a future Stage C archetype loader) can resolve `--party=no-maneuvers-v1` back to the real object instead of every caller hand-rolling its own lookup — covered by a direct test.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Stage C (the archetype library itself — cautious/aggressive/flanker/mindless/zealot profiles; `personnel-mirror.mjs` and `tournament-runner.mjs` are ready to receive them the moment they exist, since both already accept arbitrary profile pairs), Stage D (automated weight search), Stage E (promotion into campaign data) — all deliberately held for their own passes. True per-INDIVIDUAL mirror rosters (right now every roster slot mirrors the same fixture on both sides at symmetric positions; asymmetric mirror rosters aren't needed yet). A `concurrency` auto-tuning pass that probes the host for its real physical-core count — `Math.floor(cpus().length / 2)` is a measured, defensible heuristic on THIS machine's own SMT layout, not a guarantee for every host; a caller on different hardware who cares should measure and override, not trust the default blindly. Further worker-pool optimization (e.g. pre-warming each worker with a throwaway battle to avoid the V8 JIT-cold-start tax visible in the elapsedMs numbers of a worker's FIRST job) — the persistent-pool fix already delivers a real, honest 2x at realistic ladder scale, and chasing more would be optimizing a tool ahead of the archetype work it exists to unblock.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/scenarios/personnel-mirror.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/tournament-worker.mjs`, `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/tournament-runner.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/personnel-mirror.test.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/tournament-runner.test.mjs`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/ai-profiles.mjs` (added `PROFILES` registry). Real CLI run appended to `analysis/battle-history.md` (2026-08-31, 50 seeds).

## 48. The Archetype Ladder, Stage C — six named archetypes, each proven to behave differently

Ingram: *"Start Stage C of the archetype ladder."* Stage C, per the plan proposed 2026-08-30: *"The archetype library. Six named profiles, each with a direction-asserting behavioural test. Immediately useful for the campaign, and doubles as the ladder's opponent pool."*

### The six, and what each one really changes

`tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/archetypes.mjs` defines six profiles, every one a MINIMAL diff from `BASELINE_V1` — only the field(s) the Ladder plan's own §05 table calls out for that archetype differ; everything else, including every other weight, stays byte-identical to the control (proven directly, field-by-field, in `archetypes.test.mjs`). Every field used was verified against real, ALREADY-CONSUMED engine code before being assigned a direction, not guessed — `aggression` and the caution weights predate this pass entirely (design doc §35/§22), `temperament`'s three flee-immune values are read directly in `checkShouldFlee` (GM Core pg. 78's own two named exceptions plus this project's own "mindless" extension), and `templates`' phase-repeat behavior (`decide.mjs`'s own `planTurn`) was confirmed by direct reading before any archetype relied on it:

- **Aggressive** — `aggression: 2.0` (doubles the divisor that dampens its own caution factor), `weights.lowHpCaution: 2.0` (a quarter of baseline). `moraleEnabled` stays at BASELINE_V1's own default (false) — "morale off" is the absence of an opt-in, not a new field.
- **Cautious** — `weights.lowHpCaution: 16.0`, `weights.outnumberedCaution: 3.0`, `moraleEnabled: true` — the one archetype that actually opts into the real disengage mechanism.
- **Flanker** — `templates: ["reposition-and-shoot", "unconstrained"]`, `weights.threatExposure: 0.35` (nearly 3x baseline).
- **Brawler** — `templates: ["double-strike", "control-then-attack"]`, verbatim, no other field touched at all.
- **Mindless** — `temperament: "mindless"`, `competence: 0.3`, `templates: []`, `moraleEnabled: true`. `templates: []` is the literal, honest expression of "no templates" the plan calls for, not a functional requirement — `competence < 1` alone already puts `decide()` on its pre-Stage-2 single-action path (whole-turn planning only runs at `competence >= 1`), and an empty template list was confirmed safe by direct reading regardless: `planTurn` returns null immediately or with zero templates, and `decide()` falls through to that same single-action path, never a crash.
- **Zealot** — `temperament: "fanatical"`, `moraleEnabled: true`. The only diff from `BASELINE_V1` at all.

**Why `moraleEnabled: true` on every temperament-immune archetype, not just "leave it off":** immunity to a mechanism that's already switched off isn't a real, testable claim. Mindless and Zealot both opt INTO the real morale mechanism specifically so their own immunity is a genuine, provable behavioral fact, not a vacuous one — this was a deliberate design decision, not an oversight, and it's exactly what makes the behavioral tests below real.

### A real bug caught by this session's own "verify before trusting" discipline

The first draft of the bloodied-retreat probe built its test combatant directly from a loaded fixture, spread with a few overrides, WITHOUT calling `applyProfileToCombatant` — and `checkShouldFlee` reads `me.temperament` off the COMBATANT object, not off `decide()`'s own options (`decideOptionsForProfile` only forwards competence/weights/templates/moraleEnabled/disabledActionTypes; `temperament`/`aggression` are combatant-level fields Stage A's own `applyProfileToCombatant` is what actually applies). Result: Mindless and Zealot both incorrectly ROUTED in the first real probe run, despite being supposedly flee-immune — because their `temperament` never reached the combatant at all. Caught immediately by reading the real probe output before writing a single assertion, fixed in the probe (never a bug in `applyProfileToCombatant` itself, which was correct all along), then re-verified clean. Exactly the discipline this project has followed all session: real output, read before trusted.

### The behavioral tests — real `runBattle` calls, same scenario/seed, only the profile differs

**Bloodied 1-vs-2** (a real Lunar-17 at 4/15 HP facing two full-HP Security Goons, `checkShouldFlee`'s own documented real trigger): Cautious actually routes (`decisionReason` cites GM Core's own "back down from a battle they're obviously losing" text, verbatim); Aggressive never even reaches the mechanism (`moraleEnabled: false`); Mindless and Zealot both reach it — mechanism ON, `moraleEnabled: true` — and are immune, any movement either makes is a real, differently-worded tactical choice, never the canned routing text. A direct, paired, real proof that temperament immunity is genuine, not an artifact of morale being off.

**Flanker vs. Brawler, 8 real seeds** (Lunar-17 vs. Security Goon, 9 squares apart, the same real distance/positions `ai-profiles.test.mjs`'s own golden sequences use): Brawler's whole-battle move count was 0 in EVERY one of 8 real seeds — a direct, code-guaranteed consequence of `double-strike`/`control-then-attack`'s own phase filters never matching "move" once at least one attack/control action is legal, confirmed empirically, not just by code reading. Flanker's total across the identical 8 seeds was 3 (non-zero in 3 of 8) — real, measured, and HONESTLY modest: this engine has no flanking-bonus or cover mechanic to chase in a wall-free arena (confirmed directly — no `flank` reference anywhere in `state-value.mjs`/`decide.mjs`), so Flanker's actual lever (`threatExposure`-driven repositioning) only pays off when moving genuinely changes exposure, which a wall-free open arena rarely offers. A real, non-cherry-picked, non-zero contrast against Brawler's real, structural 0 — not the dramatic difference a literal "flanking bonus" might have produced, reported exactly as measured rather than inflated.

### Real tournament results — the archetypes actually work, at ladder scale

Three real 30-seed mirror tournaments (`tournament-runner.mjs`, Lunar-17+Security Goon roster), appended to `battle-history.md`:

- **baseline-v1 vs. mindless-v1: 96.7% / 3.3%.** A strong, real confirmation of this project's own standing AI-strength philosophy — Mindless isn't a weaker VERSION of the same intelligence, it's a genuinely worse-playing archetype (low competence, no whole-turn lookahead), and the numbers show it decisively. Exactly the sanctioned, narratively-legitimate way to field a weaker-playing combatant without it reading as a general AI failure.
- **aggressive-v1 vs. cautious-v1: 86.7% / 0.0%** (13.3% timeout) — a real, slightly counterintuitive finding, reported honestly rather than smoothed over: in a SYMMETRIC mirror match with matched speeds, Cautious's own real disengage behavior doesn't deny the aggressor a follow-up, so retreating reads as a liability here rather than a survival strategy. Worth remembering before assuming "cautious" means "harder to beat."
- **brawler-v1 vs. flanker-v1: 26.7% / 0.0%** (73.3% timeout) — both archetypes are slow to force a resolution against each other; a real, honestly-reported result, not dug into further (that kind of investigation is Stage D's territory, not Stage C's).

**⚠ CORRECTION (§49):** all three of these numbers were measured through the same real `personnel-mirror.mjs` confound §49 documents and fixes — left unedited here as a historical record; see §49 for the real, re-measured numbers (mindless-v1's finding held up almost exactly; the other two moved substantially, one direction even flipping). The bloodied-retreat and Flanker-vs-Brawler BEHAVIORAL tests just above this section are unaffected — they never used `personnel-mirror.mjs`, only real, correctly-labeled party/opposition scenarios built by hand.

### Verification

`test/battle-simulator/archetypes.test.mjs` (new, 17 tests): completeness/frozen-ness for all six, a field-by-field byte-identical-except-the-documented-diff proof for each archetype against `BASELINE_V1`, the `ARCHETYPES`/`ALL_PROFILES` registries, the five bloodied-retreat behavioral tests, and the two Flanker-vs-Brawler move-usage tests. `ai-profiles.mjs`'s own `PROFILES` registry is unchanged; `archetypes.mjs` imports it and re-exports `ALL_PROFILES` (`{...PROFILES, ...ARCHETYPES}`) as the one complete registry a caller resolves any id against — deliberately one-directional (archetypes.mjs depends on ai-profiles.mjs, never the reverse) so the two files never import each other. `tournament-runner.mjs`'s CLI updated to resolve `--party`/`--opposition` against `ALL_PROFILES` instead of the narrower `PROFILES`, so every archetype is immediately usable with zero other changes — proven directly by the three real tournament runs above. Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Stage D (automated weight search against the league + this archetype pool), Stage E (promotion into campaign data — an `ai-profile` schema, a `profileRef` on NPCs). A dedicated "archetype battery" script running all six against the control in one combined report — deliberately NOT built this pass: Stage C's own real deliverable is the six profiles plus their direction-asserting tests, and the three real tournament runs above already prove the archetypes work end-to-end through the existing CLI with no new infrastructure; a proper battery belongs closer to Stage D, once there's a real automated-search consumer for it. Digging into WHY brawler-v1/flanker-v1 timeout so often against each other, or WHY cautious-v1 loses so badly to aggressive-v1 in a mirror — real, honestly-reported findings, not investigated further; that's exactly the kind of question Stage D's automated search or a future analysis pass would chase, not a Stage C concern. True per-individual archetype assignment within a single side (right now a whole scenario side shares one profile, same right-sized scope Stage A already established).

## 49. The Archetype Ladder, Stage D — automated weight search, and a real confound found and fixed in the process

Ingram: *"Start Stage D of the archetype ladder."* Stage D, per the plan proposed 2026-08-30: *"Automated weight search. Overnight hill-climb against league + archetypes, with a promotion rule that requires beating several checkpoints. Larger · only worth it once B makes battles cheap."*

### The mechanism

`tools/battle-simulator/analysis/weight-search.mjs` — lives in `analysis/`, not `personnel-engine/`, since it's built on `tournament-runner.mjs`'s own mirror-match infrastructure and putting it in the core engine directory would invert that layering. Implements exactly the weight half of the plan's own §08 split ("thirteen scalars, continuous, automatable" — the OTHER half, templates/temperament/the eventual blackboard, stays "discrete, human-designed... explored by hand," untouched here):

- **`mutateWeights`** — exactly ONE of `DEFAULT_WEIGHTS`' 13 keys, perturbed by a random multiplicative factor in [0.6, 1.6], via the shared seeded `makeRng` (`engine/rng.mjs`) so the whole search is reproducible, never `Math.random()`.
- **The promotion rule** (`shouldPromote`) directly implements two of the Ladder plan's own §06 risk fixes: a candidate must beat EVERY member of a growing LEAGUE (every past promoted checkpoint, not just the newest — risk 2, "intransitive cycles") at a real win-rate bar (55%, clearly above a coin flip) AND win a real MAJORITY against the full Stage C archetype pool (risk 3, "overfitting the control" — tuning only against one frozen opponent teaches exploitation of that opponent specifically).
- **`runWeightSearch`** — classic single-parent hill-climbing: each generation mutates fresh from the current best (a rejected candidate is discarded, never accumulated into a drifting chain); a real promotion both becomes the new `currentBest` AND a permanent league member every future candidate must also keep beating.

### A result that looked too good to be real — and was

The first real 10-generation demonstration run promoted EVERY SINGLE candidate, 10 for 10, win rate against `baseline-v1` climbing to 93.8% by the end. Genuinely suspicious on its face — a random single-key perturbation, in either direction, on an already-reasonably-tuned baseline, succeeding 100% of the time is not what a real local search near a real optimum looks like. Checked the obvious null hypothesis directly: `baseline-v1` vs. ITSELF, in a real 40-seed `personnel-mirror.mjs` tournament — should be as close to a 50/50 coin flip as this engine produces. It came back **82.5% / 5.0%**. The identical profile on both sides. Something structural, nothing to do with any weight.

### The real root cause: `side` silently carried two different meanings

`conditions.mjs`'s `isDead`/`isUnconscious`, and `engine.mjs`'s `applyDyingTransition`, all keyed the real SF2E PC-vs-NPC dying/death asymmetry (Player Core pg. 402: PCs get the Dying condition and a chance to stabilize; ordinary NPCs just die outright at 0 HP — design doc §25's own deliberate, RAW-correct implementation) off the literal string `combatant.side === "party"`. Everywhere else in this project, that's exactly correct — `side` genuinely means "is this a real PC" in every asymmetric PC-vs-NPC scenario this engine has ever run. But `personnel-mirror.mjs` (§47) repurposes `side` as an arbitrary MIRROR SLOT LABEL — the identical fixture deployed once as "party," once as "opposition," specifically so only the AI profile differs. The engine had no way to know that, so the "opposition" copy of every mirror-match combatant was silently dying outright at 0 HP while its "party" mirror-twin kept the real dying/recovery spiral — a pure artifact of which slot it happened to land in, not anything about the two AI profiles being compared. Every tournament number reported anywhere in §47 or §48 was measured through this confound.

### The fix — additive, backward-compatible, grounded in the rule's own text

Player Core's own quoted text already anticipates exactly this override: *"The GM might determine that villains, powerful monsters, special NPCs... can use these rules as well."* `conditions.mjs` gained one new function, `usesDyingRules(combatant)`, returning `combatant.pcDeathRules ?? combatant.side === "party"` — `undefined` (every existing fixture, every existing scenario) reproduces today's exact side-keyed behavior byte-for-byte; an explicit `pcDeathRules: true`/`false` overrides it. `isDead`, `isUnconscious`, and `applyDyingTransition` all now read through this one function instead of re-deriving the check inline (they were the only three real call sites — confirmed by a direct grep across the whole engine before touching anything, not assumed). `personnel-mirror.mjs` now sets `pcDeathRules: true` explicitly on BOTH deployed copies of every roster fixture, regardless of slot — chosen over "neither side gets it" because these are the SAME fixtures that get real dying/recovery treatment in every other scenario in this project (Lunar-17's own "Transfer Vitality" heal ability exists specifically to counter the dying spiral — denying that to its "opposition" mirror-copy would understate a real part of its kit), and because a longer, more decision-rich fight gives the AI comparison more real signal than one lucky first knockout.

Win/loss OUTCOME detection (`engine.mjs`'s own `const party = [...state.combatants.values()].filter((c) => c.side === "party")`) was deliberately left untouched — that's a genuinely different concern (which slot won THIS fight), correctly still keyed on the literal `side` field.

**Re-verified**: the identical `baseline-v1`-vs-itself control check, same 40 seeds, post-fix: **22.5% / 22.5%** at `roundLimit: 30` (the round limit `personnel-mirror.mjs`/`tournament-runner.mjs` both default to now — see below for why 20 stopped being enough).

### A second real, honest consequence: fights got longer

With `pcDeathRules` symmetric, fights run measurably longer (both sides can survive a near-lethal hit and fight on) — the same 40-seed control check at the OLD `roundLimit: 20` default left 75.0% of battles as an uninformative timeout post-fix. Measured directly rather than guessed at three round limits (20/30/40); 30 was the real, chosen middle ground — a genuinely symmetric 22.5%/22.5% split with a usable 55% decisive rate, versus 40's slightly better 57.5% decisive rate at real extra cost. `personnel-mirror.mjs`'s own default, `tournament-runner.mjs`'s own default (both `runTournamentParallel` and `runTournamentSequential` had their OWN separate `= 20` default that silently shadowed the scenario builder's — found and fixed at the same time), its CLI's `--roundLimit` default, and `weight-search.mjs`'s own new `roundLimit` param all moved to 30 together.

### Every §47/§48 tournament number, re-measured

| Comparison | Original (confounded) | Corrected | What changed |
|---|---|---|---|
| baseline-v1 vs. baseline-v1 (control) | 82.5% / 5.0% | 22.5% / 22.5% | Confound fully explained — a truly symmetric mirror now reads as a coin flip |
| baseline-v1 vs. no-maneuvers-v1 | 80.0% / 10.0% | 32.0% / 16.0% (52.0% timeout) | Direction held (maneuvers still help, ~2:1) — magnitude was substantially inflated |
| baseline-v1 vs. mindless-v1 | 96.7% / 3.3% | 90.0% / 10.0% | Barely moved — a blowout this decisive resolves well within the round limit regardless of the bug |
| aggressive-v1 vs. cautious-v1 | 86.7% / 0.0% | 56.7% / 10.0% (33.3% timeout) | Direction held (aggressive still clearly ahead) — cautious was never actually hopeless, just disadvantaged |
| brawler-v1 vs. flanker-v1 | 26.7% / 0.0% (73.3% timeout) | 0.0% / 3.3% (96.7% timeout) | The apparent "brawler advantage" was almost ENTIRELY the bug — corrected, this pairing barely ever resolves |

The general pattern, stated honestly: the confound's real impact scaled with how CLOSE a fight already was. A genuine blowout (mindless-v1) resolves so fast the extra survivability barely matters. A close, grindy fight (brawler-v1 vs. flanker-v1) was almost entirely explained by it.

### The real, corrected Stage D demonstration

Same real 10-generation run, same search seed, post-fix, real (not lowered) promotion thresholds: **0 of 10 generations promoted.** Every candidate's win rate against `baseline-v1` landed in a real, plausible 25%-50% range — below the 55% bar, exactly what a legitimate local search near an already-reasonably-tuned point should look like most of the time. This is the honest, expected result at session-scoped demonstration scale (the plan's own §09 explicitly scopes Stage D as needing "overnight," not ten generations) — the mechanism works correctly; finding a real improvement needs the actual larger run this pass was never trying to be.

### Verification

`test/battle-simulator/weight-search.test.mjs` (21 tests total): `mutateWeights`'s exactly-one-key/bounded-factor/deterministic/never-mutates-input guarantees, `shouldPromote` against synthetic win-rate data covering every branch (both promotion-rule halves, both pass and fail, the empty-league and empty-archetype-pool edges), real, small `evaluateCandidate`/`runWeightSearch` integration tests — including one with a deliberately near-zero promotion bar proving the PROMOTION code path itself fires on real battle data, not just on synthetic `shouldPromote` input — the `deadlineAt` safety valve (an already-passed deadline stops immediately, a generous one never trips, omitting it behaves exactly as before), and (2026-09-01 addendum) `resumeState`'s own byte-identical-continuation proof plus a real check that a resumed generation faces the exact restored league, not just the original starting profile. `conditions.mjs`/`engine.mjs`'s `usesDyingRules` change is additive and defaults to exactly today's behavior for every existing caller — proven by the full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean afterward (every pre-existing test, including every golden-sequence test that pins an exact real action sequence, passed unmodified). `personnel-mirror.test.mjs`/`tournament-runner.test.mjs`/`archetypes.test.mjs` all re-run clean against the `roundLimit` default change too (none of them relied on an implicit default — every test already passed its own explicit value).

### Addendum (2026-09-01) — the real overnight run, an unrelated machine restart, and real checkpoint/resume support

Ingram: *"Run the real overnight weight search."* Launched for real: up to 2000 generations, `n=24` seeds/matchup (a real calibration run measured ~31s/generation at that scale before committing), bounded by a genuine 3-hour wall-clock `deadlineAt` — the safety valve this section's own main text already built, now exercised for real rather than just tested. **The run reached generation 187 (~99 real minutes) with 0 promotions before an unrelated machine restart killed the process.** No bug in the search itself — a real, external interruption, but a real gap this project's own tooling had no answer for: the search had no way to pick back up except from scratch, discarding 99 real minutes of legitimate work.

**The fix, built before relaunching**: `runWeightSearch` gained `resumeState: { league, completedGenerations }`. Restoring the league is trivial (its members are plain, JSON-round-trippable objects). The one real subtlety is the RNG — `mutateWeights` draws from the shared seeded stream EXACTLY twice per call (confirmed by direct reading, not assumed: one draw for which key, one for the factor), so resuming correctly means fast-forwarding a fresh `makeRng(searchSeed)` by exactly `2 × completedGenerations` discarded calls before the loop starts, landing on the IDENTICAL internal state an uninterrupted run would have reached. Verified directly, not just reasoned about: a real test runs a search straight through for 4 generations, separately runs the first 2 and then resumes for the remaining 2, and asserts the resumed generations 3-4 are BYTE-IDENTICAL (same mutated key, same factor, same verdict) to what the uninterrupted run produced for its own generations 3-4.

The CLI now writes a real checkpoint (`tools/battle-simulator/logs/weight-search-checkpoint.json`, gitignored — ephemeral working state, never campaign data) after every single generation, and `--resume=true` loads it — refusing to resume, loudly, if the saved config (roster/seedSpec/roundLimit/searchSeed/archetype pool) doesn't match what's being requested, so an accidental config change can't silently continue a different experiment under the same name. `generations` itself is deliberately EXCLUDED from that match check — raising the target ceiling is the entire point of a resume, not a sign of a mismatched experiment (a real bug caught by a real smoke test: the first draft included `generations` in the match key, so extending 3→6 generations across a resume incorrectly refused as "different config" — fixed, then re-verified both that a legitimate ceiling change resumes cleanly and that an ACTUAL mismatch, like a different `searchSeed`, still correctly refuses).

**The relaunch, and the real final result.** Restarted with a fresh `searchSeed=2` (rather than replaying the same first 187 generations `searchSeed=1` would have deterministically reproduced) — two independent sweeps from the same starting point instead of one continuous run, broader real coverage either way. This run survived a SECOND real interruption — this one just a Claude Code session restart, not a machine restart — completely unaffected, because the search itself runs as a detached background process independent of the session that launched it; only the session's own *watch* on it needed re-arming. It completed cleanly on its own 3-hour deadline: **320 generations in 180.3 minutes, 0 promotions.** Combined real total across both runs: **507 generations, 0 promotions** — the strongest real confirmation yet that `BASELINE_V1`'s hand-tuned weights sit in a solid part of the search space. Per-weight analysis of run 2's own fully-logged 320 generations found two real standouts (`closing`, `enemyDamage` — both topped out at a genuine 50% win rate against the baseline, the closest either got) and, more surprisingly, five weights (`resource`, `outnumberedCaution`, `tempoDenial`, `mobilityDenial`, `objective`) that produced the EXACT SAME win rate on every one of 18-32 independent trials each, regardless of mutation magnitude — verified directly against the raw log, not assumed. Full results, a reconstructed real battle from the closest candidate (`closing` ×1.435 vs. baseline-v1, a real 29-round mirror match with two separate near-death moments for the same caster), and a real cross-battle action-diversity tally (5,609 real actions, 9 distinct action types, mean 3.12 types per round) are written up as a standalone report: [The Overnight Search](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f1f40d08-07ab-4d12-8fcd-9aeb7e59a924).

**Verifying the "flat five," and a real efficiency fix (2026-09-01).** Ingram, reviewing the report: *"Verify outnumberedCaution and objective before anything else - take this as a chance to improve the simulation based on the new information."* Checked both directly, not extrapolated from `resource`'s own already-confirmed mechanism:

- **`objective` is the EXACT same story as `resource`.** `state-value.mjs`'s own scoring only applies `weights.objective` to a combatant with a real `.objective` field set (`if (!c.objective) continue`) — grepped the default roster's fixtures, `personnel-mirror.mjs`'s own scenario builder, and every one of the 8 current profiles: zero matches, anywhere. Mirror matches have no battle-map-objective concept at all, so this weight is mechanically guaranteed inert for this whole evaluation harness as it currently exists, exactly like `resource`.
- **`outnumberedCaution` is genuinely different — real, and sensitive, just apparently never load-bearing here.** Reproduced `evaluateState` directly on a real down-ally position (one side's Security Goon at 0 HP, its Lunar-17 deciding, both enemies still able to threaten): the real score moved from -24.7048 (×0.6) to -25.8328 (×1.6) — a genuine ~1.1-point swing, nothing like `resource`/`objective`'s dead flatline. A control check with the ally alive confirmed the OTHER half of the mechanism too: score is *exactly* identical across the same three factors (3.8970 every time) when the visible-enemy-to-visible-ally ratio can't exceed 1.0, matching the code's own real math (`Math.max(0, outnumberedRatio - 1)`) precisely. So the term fires correctly and produces a real, measurable score difference when its precondition holds — it just never seems to end up being the actual deciding factor between two competing actions in this specific roster's own 24-seed sample. A genuinely open question, not a solved one — flagged honestly as still-unverified-further rather than folded into the same explanation as the other two.

**The real improvement**: mutating `resource` or `objective` is proven, guaranteed wasted search effort for the default roster — roughly 2 of every 13 generations (~15%) produce a candidate that cannot possibly score any differently than its parent, no matter the mutation factor. `mutateWeights`/`runWeightSearch` gained an optional `excludeKeys` parameter (default: none, zero behavior change for any existing caller — the completed 507-generation run stays exactly reproducible by anyone calling these functions directly with the same `searchSeed`), and the CLI's own default now excludes `PROVEN_INERT_FOR_DEFAULT_ROSTER` unless overridden with `--excludeKeys=`. `tempoDenial`/`mobilityDenial` — the other two "flat" weights — were deliberately NOT included in that constant yet: both were suspected but not yet independently confirmed the same rigorous way, and excluding them without doing the same real check first would be exactly the kind of extrapolation this fix itself was a correction of.

**`tempoDenial` and `mobilityDenial`, verified the same way (2026-09-01, same day).** Ingram: *"Verify tempoDenial and mobilityDenial the same way - have we improved things or are they much the same?"*

- **`tempoDenial` is the third proven-mechanically-impossible case.** `tempoOwed(combatant)` is `combatant.stunnedValue ?? 0` — Stunned only. Neither fixture has any stun-inflicting trait/ability anywhere (grepped directly, zero matches), and nothing in `engine.mjs` ever sets `stunnedValue` for either of them. Added to `PROVEN_INERT_FOR_DEFAULT_ROSTER`, now `["resource", "objective", "tempoDenial"]` — roughly 3 of 13 generations (~23%) were wasted before this fix, now zero are.
- **`mobilityDenial` is real, working, and deliberately NOT excluded — a genuinely different, more interesting case.** A constructed state (enemy Security Goon grappled, both of MY combatants positioned beyond its own weapon range) showed a real ~6-point score swing across the tested factor range, proving the code path fires correctly. But the SAME construction with only the grappler moved back adjacent (the realistic case — Grapple requires melee range to attempt in the first place) showed the same flat, zero-sensitivity result as everything else: the grappler is, by definition, always within the grappled target's own reach the instant the grapple lands, which permanently satisfies this term's own disqualifying "already in range" check. The ONLY way `mobilityDenial` can ever score is if the grappler ALSO retreats far away while the grapple still holds — a tactical sequence a sensible AI essentially never produces. Self-defeating given how this roster actually plays, not structurally impossible the way the other three genuinely are — so it stays real, un-excluded, and honestly flagged as "probably never fires here, but for a reason about tactics rather than physics."

**A live demonstration of exactly why the earlier statistical-power concern was real, not theoretical.** A quick n=4 smoke test of the new 3-key default produced a real promotion — `enemyDamage` ×0.662, 75.0% vs. `baseline-v1` at n=4, comfortably clearing the 55% bar. Reconstructed the exact same candidate and re-evaluated it at the real n=24 standard: **37.5% vs. `baseline-v1`** — a real loss, not a lead. Pure small-sample noise, caught before it went anywhere, and the clearest possible confirmation that n=24 (this project's own real standard throughout) is a floor worth respecting, not a number picked for convenience.

Verified end to end: 300 real draws with all three keys excluded never selects any of them; excluding every real key throws a clear error instead of silently drawing `undefined`; a real 6-generation CLI run confirms the new default (`excluding [resource, objective, tempoDenial]` printed up front) never mutates any of the three across any real generation.

**Where this leaves "are the bots getting smarter": not yet, but the search is smarter about where to look.** No real, n=24-verified promotion exists anywhere in this project's history — every apparent lead (including today's) either capped below the bar or evaporated under a real sample size. What HAS accumulated across 507+ generations and three separate verification passes: 3 of 13 weights are proven, mechanically dead for this roster (now correctly skipped); 2 more (`outnumberedCaution`, `mobilityDenial`) are proven real but apparently never decisive here, for two DIFFERENT, now-understood reasons; and `closing`/`enemyDamage` keep independently resurfacing as the two real leads across multiple separate runs (the original 507-generation search, and both of today's quick 15-generation checks) — a consistent signal worth a future search actually targeting, rather than spreading effort evenly across all 13 dials.

**A real multi-key search, and the closest real near-miss yet (2026-09-01/02).** Ingram: *"Run a targeted search on closing and enemyDamage together."* `mutateWeightsTogether(baseWeights, rng, targetKeys)` is a new, real, general primitive — not a one-off script — that perturbs EVERY key in `targetKeys` simultaneously, each with its own independently-drawn factor, plugged into the existing `runWeightSearch` loop via a new `targetKeys` option (when set, every generation mutates that fixed set together instead of drawing one random key). The one real subtlety: `mutateWeights` always draws exactly 2 rng values per generation (one key-selection, one factor), but `mutateWeightsTogether` draws exactly `targetKeys.length` (no key-selection draw at all, since the keys are fixed) — `runWeightSearch`'s own resume fast-forward math now computes `drawsPerGeneration` per mode instead of a hardcoded `2`, verified directly: a real 2-key targeted search resumed after 2 generations matches an uninterrupted 4-generation run byte-for-byte on generations 3-4, same proof style as the original single-key resume fix.

Real run: 50 generations, `n=24`, `targetKeys: ["closing", "enemyDamage"]`, 25.3 real minutes, 0 promotions. The closest result by a wide margin — generation 42, `closing` ×1.4084 / `enemyDamage` ×0.8883 — hit 54.2% vs. `baseline-v1` with 5/6 archetypes beaten, a hair under the 55% bar. Checked before believing it, the same discipline the earlier n=4 false alarm established: re-evaluated the identical reconstructed candidate at double the sample (`n=48`) — **54.2% dropped to 50.0%**, archetypes beaten dropped to 4/6. Regression to a coin flip, not a real, durable edge; the closest real near-miss this project has found still doesn't survive a harder look. A real, visible DIRECTIONAL pattern across the full 50 generations, reported honestly as an observation rather than a proven law: results pushing `closing` UP while pulling `enemyDamage` DOWN (or leaving it near baseline) consistently scored better than results moving both the same direction — not itself promotion-worthy, but a real, data-grounded hint about which corner of the joint space is actually promising, worth a future search's own attention.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Stage E (promotion into campaign data) — since built, see §50. CMA-ES or any more sophisticated search algorithm than hill-climbing (single- or multi-key) — the plan's own §08 lists these as real alternatives, still not attempted. Actually persisting a promoted search checkpoint as a new permanent exported profile in `ai-profiles.mjs`/`archetypes.mjs` — deliberately left as data the search itself returns/logs, never auto-written into a source file as a new "official" profile; that's a real human decision, not something this automated loop should do unilaterally. Investigating brawler-v1-vs-flanker-v1's now near-100% timeout rate further, or lengthening `roundLimit`/seed counts to shrink the (now honestly much wider) uncertainty on the no-maneuvers/aggressive-cautious corrected numbers — real, honestly-reported open questions, not chased down this pass. Stitching a resumed run's FULL per-generation table (pre- and post-interruption) into one combined report — the league/final-best summary is always complete across a resume, but the markdown table itself only lists the newly-run segment. Finding out WHY `outnumberedCaution` never seems to be load-bearing despite being real — still open. Trying OTHER key pairs/triples together (the targeted-search capability now generalizes to any set, not just `closing`+`enemyDamage`), or a genuinely larger multi-key search budget aimed specifically at the "closing up, enemyDamage down" direction this pass's own data pointed toward — natural next steps, not started here.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/weight-search.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/weight-search.test.mjs`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/conditions.mjs` (`usesDyingRules`, `isDead`/`isUnconscious` rewritten through it), `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/engine.mjs` (`applyDyingTransition`'s own check), `tools/battle-simulator/scenarios/personnel-mirror.mjs` (`pcDeathRules: true` on both slots, `roundLimit` default 20→30), `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/tournament-runner.mjs` (matching `roundLimit` default fix, in three places that had silently drifted out of sync; `resumeState`/checkpoint read-write, `excludeKeys`/`PROVEN_INERT_FOR_DEFAULT_ROSTER` — three keys — and, as of 2026-09-01/02, `mutateWeightsTogether`/`targetKeys` all added to `weight-search.mjs`'s own CLI). Real corrected CLI runs appended to `battle-history.md` (2026-08-31): the baseline-vs-baseline control check at three round limits, all five corrected §47/§48 comparisons, and the real 0-promotion Stage D demonstration, plus (2026-09-01/02) the real 50-generation targeted search. Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean after every change in this section, all four 2026-09-01/02 addenda included.

## 50. The Archetype Ladder, Stage E — a `profileRef` field, deliberately without a matching data-authoring workflow

Ingram: *"Start Stage E of the archetype ladder."* Stage E, per the plan proposed 2026-08-30: *"Promotion into campaign data. An `ai-profile` schema and a `profileRef` on NPCs, once the archetypes have proven themselves in simulation. Deferred · don't build ahead of need."* The archetypes have now been through Stage C's own direction-asserting behavioral proof and Stage D's real tournament measurement — the "once proven" condition is met.

### What this deliberately is NOT

The plan's own phrase "an `ai-profile` schema" invites a natural but wrong reading: one JSON file per profile under `/data`, mirroring the JS objects (`BASELINE_V1`, the six archetypes), the way every OTHER entity in this project gets a hand-authored data file matching a schema. That would be a mistake here specifically. Checked against this project's own stated architecture before writing anything: ROADMAP.md #1/#2 describes the Foundry sync pipeline as syncing `/data` into real Foundry documents (Scenes, Actors, JournalEntries) — and a Foundry Actor has no concept of "AI weights" or "turn-template preferences" at all (per `design/foundry-technical-reference.md`'s own already-researched sf2e data model). Duplicating a profile's real numbers into `/data` JSON would create a second copy of something that already has exactly one correct home — the already-built, already-tested `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/ai-profiles.mjs`/`archetypes.mjs` — with no consumer that would ever read the copy, and a real, live drift risk identical in shape to the one `ai-profiles.mjs`'s own header already warns about for `BASELINE_V1`'s frozen weights snapshot.

### What it actually is: one field, pointing OUT of `/data`

`data/schema/common/sf2e-common.schema.json`'s `combatStatblock` — the one real, shared shape both `npc.schema.json`'s optional `statblock` and `creature.schema.json` (every Claimant) already `$ref` — gained one new optional field: `profileRef` (string or null). Unlike every other `*Ref` field in this schema, it does NOT point at another `/data` JSON file; it's the id of a real profile from the battle-simulator's own `ALL_PROFILES` registry (`archetypes.mjs`, which merges `ai-profiles.mjs`'s two control profiles with all six archetypes). Deliberately NOT a JSON Schema `enum` of current ids: an enum would need hand-updating every time a new archetype is added (Stage C already established this as a growing library) and would silently go stale the moment it didn't — a plain string with a description pointing at the live registry can't drift out of sync the same way, because it never claimed to enumerate anything in the first place.

**What it's actually for**: purely descriptive metadata, for a human (or a future encounter-validation tool built on ROADMAP #3) to record "when this creature needs to fight in the simulator, play it like THIS" — e.g. a future Claimant file could carry `"profileRef": "aggressive-v1"` to note it should be run through the battle-simulator as the Aggressive archetype when tuning its difficulty. Never read by the Foundry sync pipeline. Never validated against a live list of real ids (no tooling exists to do that, and building one now would be getting ahead of there being any real data that uses the field at all).

### Why no real data file sets this yet

No real Claimant instances exist yet (`data/antagonists/claimants/` currently holds only `claimant-guidelines.json` — build rules, not individual stat blocks), and inventing one specifically to demonstrate this field would be exactly the kind of unprompted creative content CLAUDE.md's own "Working on this project" section reserves for the user. The field is real, tested, and ready; using it on a real antagonist is a natural next step for whoever builds this campaign's first real Claimant, not something to manufacture here.

### Verification

A real, previously-existing test this pass leaned on rather than reinvented: `test/tier2-data-corpus.test.mjs` already runs every real `/data` file through AJV against its declared `$schema` (a real, live validator — CLAUDE.md's own "not currently enforced by any validator" note turns out to be stale for at least this file, left as its own small correction rather than silently ignored). That test re-ran clean, confirming the new field doesn't break any existing real data file. One new test added to the same file: `combatStatblock`'s own `profileRef` field, compiled directly via AJV, validated against EVERY real id currently in `ALL_PROFILES` (not a hand-picked sample — the live registry itself, so a future archetype addition is automatically covered), plus `null` and omitted (both must pass, the field is optional) and a non-string/non-null value (must be REJECTED, proving the type constraint is real and not just decorative documentation).

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Any real Claimant/NPC data file actually setting `profileRef` — deliberately left for whoever creates the campaign's first real antagonist that needs one. A validator that cross-checks a real `profileRef` value against the live `ALL_PROFILES` registry at data-corpus-test time (this pass's own new test checks the reverse direction — every real id validates against the schema — not "every used ref resolves to a real id," since nothing uses the field yet to check). Extending `profileRef` to `antagonist.schema.json` — the plan's own language says "NPCs," and antagonists are purely narrative with no `combatStatblock` of their own to attach the field to; adding one would mean restructuring that schema beyond what's actually being asked for. Any tooling to auto-generate or auto-sync a `/data` copy of the JS profile objects — deliberately rejected, not just deferred, for the drift reasons above.

### Files

Changed: `data/schema/common/sf2e-common.schema.json` (`profileRef` added to `combatStatblock`), `test/tier2-data-corpus.test.mjs` (one new test, plus a real import of `ALL_PROFILES`). No new files — Stage E's entire real deliverable is one schema field and the test proving it works, exactly matching its own "small, don't build ahead of need" scope once the actual precondition (archetypes proven in simulation) was met.

## 51. Anchor baselines — an absolute floor, not just relative comparisons

Ingram, after reviewing the Overnight Search report: *"It feels hard to know if we actually have a very strong SF2E bot or an extremely bad one... The only one I'd like to add to 'worst' option bot and 'random' valid option bot. We could test against both of those and learn something too right?"*

### The real gap this closes

Every single measurement this whole Archetype Ladder effort has produced — 507+ weight-search generations, every archetype tournament, the targeted `closing`/`enemyDamage` search — is RELATIVE: is candidate X better than `baseline-v1`, does archetype A beat archetype B. None of it is ABSOLUTE. Nothing had ever asked "compared to a bot that's actually trying to lose, or one with no strategy at all, how good is `decide()` really?" Three real anchor bots now answer that, each a genuinely different KIND of bad:

- **`decideRandom`** — zero intelligence, uniform pick among whatever's legal, no bias in any direction.
- **`decideWorst`** — actively adversarial, but adversarial according to THIS project's own real `evaluateState` — reuses the exact same scored-candidate machinery `decide()`'s own competence<1 fallback already uses, and picks the LOWEST-gain legal option instead of the highest. Doubles as a sanity check on the evaluator itself: if `decide()` didn't beat this by an even wider margin than it beats Random, that would be real evidence the evaluator isn't actually separating good play from bad.
- **`decideNearestOnly`** — a real, honest heuristic, not malice or chance: always go after the closest living enemy, attack it if in range, otherwise move toward it, nothing else. One club-footed idea, applied with total consistency.

All three (`tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/baselines.mjs`) reuse `generateProposals`/`forkTurnSandbox` exactly as `decide()` does — legality is still never re-derived, only ever confirmed by the real engine in a real sandbox. Wrapped as profile-SHAPED objects (`RANDOM_BASELINE`/`WORST_BASELINE`/`NEAREST_ONLY_BASELINE`, real ids `random-baseline`/`worst-baseline`/`nearest-only-baseline`) so they slot into the existing `ALL_PROFILES` registry and the whole tournament/report pipeline with zero special-casing beyond one real dispatch point: `personnel-mirror.mjs`'s own `ai` function checks a new `decisionMode` field before falling through to the ordinary `decide()` path every real weighted profile still uses unchanged.

### Real n=24 calibration results

| Opponent | `baseline-v1` win rate | Mean rounds |
|---|---|---|
| `random-baseline` | 95.8% | 7.5 |
| `worst-baseline` | 100.0% | 5.7 |
| `nearest-only-baseline` | 100.0% | 5.6 |

`worst-baseline` losing at least as badly as `random-baseline` (100.0% vs. 95.8%) is exactly the real evaluator-sanity signal this was built to check for, and it held up at real scale, not just in the small hand-built unit test that first proved the mechanism.

**The genuinely interesting result: `nearest-only-baseline` lost just as completely as the other two (100.0%/0.0%), not investigated as a passing curiosity but checked directly.** A real transcript (seed 1) shows exactly why: the whole battle was 21 real actions, every single one a `strike` — zero moves, zero heals, zero maneuvers, the entire fight. `decideNearestOnly`'s own logic has no path to ever choose `healAbility` at all (it only ever considers attacking or moving toward the nearest enemy) — against an opponent that heals liberally (Transfer Vitality, ~27% of `baseline-v1`'s own real action mix per the Overnight Search report's own diversity tally), never healing at all is a severe, fight-losing handicap regardless of how reasonable the targeting choice itself is. Not a bug — exactly what "one plan, applied with total consistency, no adaptation" should produce — but a real, specific, now-understood reason, not a mystery, and a useful reminder that "keeps itself alive" turns out to matter as much in this evaluator as "picks good targets."

### Verification

`test/battle-simulator/baselines.test.mjs` (13 tests): each decision function proven to behave as named — Random spreads across real legal options rather than secretly always picking one (and is deterministic given the same seed), Worst is checked against an INDEPENDENTLY computed real lowest-gain option (not just "isn't the best"), Nearest-Only is proven to actually close distance when out of range and never pick a farther target over a nearer attackable one (a real test-authoring mistake caught and fixed along the way: the first attempt used a gap that was still within Security Goon's own real 180ft Arc Pistol range, so "should move" was actually wrong — fixed by using a distance confirmed beyond any real legal attack, not just "seems far"). The real, load-bearing behavioral tests — `baseline-v1` decisively beats all three in real mirror-match battles, and Worst loses at least as badly as Random — proven with real battles at small scale in the test suite and reconfirmed at the real n=24 standard via the CLI. `test/battle-simulator/archetypes.test.mjs`'s own pre-existing `ALL_PROFILES` completeness test updated to account for the three new registry entries (a real, expected failure caught by the very next full-suite run, not silently missed).

### What wasn't attempted this pass

The tactical-puzzle test suite and the systematic transcript audit — the other two real asks from the same conversation, deliberately sequenced AFTER the anchor bots since they needed the floor established first; not started this pass. A fourth "in-between" anchor (nearest-only, but heals when critically low) — the current nearest-only bot's own 0% win rate turned out to be informative exactly as built (one plan, no adaptation, period), and changing it without being asked would trade a clean, honest floor for a muddier one. Extending `ANCHOR_BASELINES` into the weight-search's own default archetype pool — deliberately left out; `runWeightSearch` still defaults to `Object.values(ARCHETYPES)` only, unchanged, so adding the anchors to `ALL_PROFILES` doesn't silently change what Stage D's own searches evaluate against.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/baselines.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/baselines.test.mjs`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/archetypes.mjs` (`ALL_PROFILES` merges in `ANCHOR_BASELINES`), `tools/battle-simulator/scenarios/personnel-mirror.mjs` (`decisionMode` dispatch in `ai`), `test/battle-simulator/archetypes.test.mjs` (`ALL_PROFILES` completeness test updated). Real n=24 CLI tournaments appended to `battle-history.md` (2026-09-02): `baseline-v1` vs. each of the three anchors. Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean (1064/1064) after every change in this section, including after the real test failure the `ALL_PROFILES` update caught and fixed.

## 52. Tactical puzzles — checking specific decisions, not just aggregate win rate

The second of Ingram's three approved testing upgrades from the same conversation as §51: "hand-picked scenarios with a known-correct answer... checked directly rather than via aggregate win rate."

### First, a real inventory check before building anything new

Before writing a single new puzzle, `test/battle-simulator/personnel-decide.test.mjs` was checked for what it already covers end-to-end against the real `decide()` function — and it turns out most of the obvious "known-correct-answer" puzzles already exist there, as regression tests tied to specific design-doc sections: retreat under `lowHpCaution` (§35), retreat when genuinely outnumbered (§22), the decisive flee override under `moraleEnabled` including both GM Core's named exceptions and this project's own "mindless" extension (§42), taking a free Grapple before attacking even though the isolated single-action gain looks worse (§43's own whole-turn-planning case, literally "a free maneuver sitting right there"), retreating to cover from a ranged threat (§26/§27), and refusing an area attack that would catch an ally in the blast (§38). Re-proving any of those here would be pure duplication, not a new deliverable — so this section only covers what's genuinely NEW.

### The real gaps, and what was built to close them

`test/battle-simulator/tactical-puzzles.test.mjs` (5 tests), every scenario built from real fixtures with HP/position/loadout overrides (the same sanctioned pattern `baselines.test.mjs`'s own `makeTwoSidedState` already uses), every puzzle run through the REAL `decide()` function BEFORE being written down as an assertion — matching this whole session's own "verify before trusting a plausible-looking result" discipline:

1. **Heal a critical ally over pressing a non-decisive attack.** A 3/25-HP ally next to a healer with an ordinary enemy in range — `decide()` heals. Verified independently, not just trusted: the test directly computes the real evaluator gain for the heal (allyDamage's own 1.0-per-HP rate on a real, guaranteed 10 HP from Transfer Vitality) against the best available attack's own real expected-damage gain, and asserts the heal wins by the actual numbers, not just by decide()'s own say-so.
2. **Heal whichever ally actually needs it, not one that's already nearly full.** Two hurt allies, 3/25 and 24/25 — `decide()` heals the 3/25 one. Deliberately built with NO enemy present at all, isolating this from puzzle 1's own heal-vs-attack question so a wrong answer could only ever have one cause.
3. **Attack the already-off-guard enemy over an equally-reachable healthy-postured one.** One prone enemy, one upright enemy, otherwise identical — `decide()` targets the prone one, correctly reading Prone's real off-guard AC penalty as a reason one shot is more likely to land than the other.
4. **"Finish the kill" — real, but conditional, not a blind low-HP rule.** This is the one that genuinely surprised the author on first hand-guess. Naive expectation: "of course it finishes the near-dead enemy, less HP to chew through." The real mechanism verified directly (both via `decide()`'s own pick and an independent side-by-side `evaluateState` comparison) is sharper than that: with a weapon strong enough to actually secure the kill (Baton, real expected damage that clears a 2-HP target outright), `decide()` prefers the near-dead target — and the real margin comes specifically from erasing that enemy's own future `threatExposure` contribution entirely (a measured ~2-point bonus on top of the ~2 raw HP banked, confirmed by directly comparing the two candidates' real evaluator gain: 4.174 for the kill vs. 2.275 for wounding the healthy target instead). Swap in Arc Pistol alone — real expected damage that does NOT clear 2 HP outright — and the preference REVERSES: `decide()` correctly targets the healthy enemy instead (real gain 1.575 vs. 1.260), because a non-lethal hit on the near-dead target gets none of that elimination bonus while still giving up real expected damage against the healthier one. The AI isn't running "attack whoever has less HP" — it's correctly pricing whether THIS hit, with THIS weapon, actually removes a threat from the board.

### What this adds, honestly

Not much statistical power — five deterministic, single-seed puzzles is not a large-sample claim, and isn't meant to be one (see §51 and the whole "verify at n=24, not n=4" thread this session already established for that kind of claim). What it adds is a different KIND of evidence, the kind aggregate win rate structurally cannot provide: proof that a SPECIFIC, nameable tactical judgment call comes out the defensible way, with the real mechanism behind it written down and independently checked, not inferred from a percentage. Every candidate scored inside `decide()` is expected-value only — `forkTurnSandbox` passes `rng: null, outcomes: EXPECTED_OUTCOMES` (engine.mjs's own header: "a sandbox can never advance the battle's real dice stream") — which is precisely why a single seed is enough here and wasn't enough for §51's win-rate claims: there is no roll-to-roll variance inside one decision to average out, only real variance across many decisions chained through many real (non-sandboxed) dice rolls in an actual battle.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

Puzzles targeting mechanisms `personnel-decide.test.mjs` already covers end-to-end (see the inventory check above) — deliberately not re-built here. A puzzle testing "prioritize the more dangerous of two equally-reachable enemies" was considered and dropped after direct analysis: with neither hit lethal, the evaluator has no term that rewards softening up the more dangerous one over the weaker one specifically (a live enemy's own outgoing threat doesn't shrink until it's actually dead or meaningfully incapacitated) — the puzzle's "known-correct answer" turned out not to be one this evaluator's own design would ever produce, so asserting it would have been testing an invented expectation, not a real one. That itself is worth recording rather than quietly dropping: target PRIORITIZATION in this engine is driven by what a hit actually accomplishes (kill, meaningful HP extraction, securing a debuff's payoff), not by the target's own danger rating in isolation.

### Files

New: `test/battle-simulator/tactical-puzzles.test.mjs`. No source changes — every puzzle passed against the existing, unmodified `decide()`/`evaluateState`. Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean after this addition.

## 53. Systematic transcript audit — and a real bug it caught on the first real run

The third of Ingram's three approved testing upgrades from the same conversation as §51/§52: "a real, non-cherry-picked sample of battle transcripts... reviewed for a pattern of bad decisions, not just one dramatic battle." This is the one that paid for itself immediately.

### What was built

`tools/battle-simulator/analysis/transcript-audit.mjs` — a real sample generator (`runAuditSample`) plus a five-rule, DECLARED-BEFORE-RUNNING selection function (`selectForManualReview`) for which battles out of that sample get a full manual transcript read: any battle with a real illegal proposal (always), the lowest-`decisionEntropy` battle per opponent, the highest-`idleTurnRate` battle per opponent (when one exists), the single longest battle in the whole sample, and a fixed, metrics-blind baseline seed for every opponent — so the choice of what to read can never quietly turn into after-the-fact cherry-picking. The actual measuring is NOT new: `metrics.mjs` (utilization/idle rate/verb diversity/decision entropy, §15a) and `action-utilization.mjs` (illegal-proposal auditing, §14j) are the same real, already-tested, scale-agnostic instruments Frame-scale has used since early in this project — confirmed compatible with personnel-scale logs completely unchanged before writing a line of new code, not assumed.

Real sample: `baseline-v1` against all 6 archetypes (§48) and all 3 anchor baselines (§51), seeds 1-6, 54 real battles.

### The real finding: 17 illegal proposals, all one bug

Every opponent showed `illegalProposals: 0` — except `cautious-v1`, which showed **17** across its 6-seed sample. Every single one was the identical shape: a `move` proposal, rejected with `"Prone — can't use this move action while prone (only Crawl and Stand are available)."`, from whichever combatant was currently routing.

Root cause, found by reading the actual rejected proposals directly: `decide()`'s decisive flee override (§42) calls `proposeFlee`, which hands back a bare `{ type: "move", to: ... }` — and, uniquely among every candidate this whole engine ever generates, WITHOUT the sandbox-legality check every other candidate in `decide.mjs` goes through before being offered (`if (!result.legal) continue`). That's deliberate for the override itself — a fleeing creature isn't supposed to re-litigate the decision against ordinary scored candidates — but it meant nothing ever verified the flee MOVE itself was legal. `cautious-v1` is the only profile in this whole sample where the override can actually fire (it's the only one with `moraleEnabled: true` and no temperament that blocks it — `mindless-v1`/`zealot-v1` both set `moraleEnabled: true` too, but their own temperament makes `checkShouldFlee` return false unconditionally, so the override never even reaches `proposeFlee` for them), which is exactly why the other five archetypes' samples were clean: nothing else in this sample could ever trigger the buggy path at all.

Real, mechanical consequence: a cautious combatant that got Tripped while already retreating would spend its FULL 3-action turn silently failing to move over and over, held for the whole turn — a real, wasted turn, on a profile whose whole point is to survive by disengaging.

### The fix

`proposeFlee` now checks `me.prone` first and returns `{ type: "stand" }` instead — a real, mechanically necessary step (Player Core: only Crawl or Stand are legal while prone), and `checkStandLegal`'s own only real gate is being alive and not grabbed/restrained, so it's unconditionally legal here. This isn't a compromise fallback: with action economy typically still remaining in the same turn, `decide()` gets called again immediately afterward with `prone` now false, at which point the override correctly returns a real move away — so a routing, tripped combatant now spends one action standing and its remaining actions actually fleeing, instead of three actions failing to do anything.

Verified: two new END TO END tests in `personnel-decide.test.mjs` (prone → Stand, chosen action independently confirmed legal via a real sandbox apply; not-prone → still a real move, proving the new prone check doesn't leak into the ordinary case). Re-ran the real audit sample after the fix: `illegalProposals` is 0 across all 9 opponents, and `cautious-v1`'s own `idleTurnRate` dropped from 5.4% to 1.9% — a real, measured improvement from fixing the bug, not just the absence of the symptom.

### The rest of the manual read

25 battles (down to 25 from 29 once the illegal-proposal flags dropped away) were read in full, plus a grep sweep across the complete transcript log for any other 0-or-negative-gain action — the cheapest possible signal for "the AI thinks this is doing nothing or actively hurting itself." Findings, honestly:

- The dominant pattern in symmetric mirror matches (identical roster both sides) is a real, sensible healing war of attrition — `lunar-17` pours Transfer Vitality into its melee ally almost every turn, `security-goon` alternates Trip-then-Strike with plain double-strikes — not degenerate play, just what a genuinely close, symmetric fight looks like, which is also why several of these run the full 30-round `roundLimit` to a timeout (consistent with §49's own finding that symmetric healing mirrors need real room to resolve).
- `worst-baseline`'s own negative/zero-gain picks throughout its transcripts are exactly correct — that IS the profile (§51), not a flaw.
- Every remaining zero-gain action was a single, isolated tail action (typically a turn's 3rd action) with nothing better available that specific micro-turn — never a repeating stuck pattern outside the one bug above.

### What wasn't attempted this pass

A larger sample (more seeds, more rosters) — 54 battles was enough to find and fix one real bug on the first run; scaling this up is cheap to do later with the exact same tool, not attempted here since nothing in this pass's own findings calls for it yet. Extending the audit to non-mirror scenarios (`personnel-complex-battle.mjs`) — deliberately out of scope; the whole mirror-match apparatus (§46) exists specifically to isolate AI quality from roster asymmetry, and this audit inherits that same reasoning.

### Files

New: `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/transcript-audit.mjs`, `test/battle-simulator/transcript-audit.test.mjs`. Changed: `tools/battle-simulator/personnel-engine/decide.mjs` (`proposeFlee`'s prone fix), `test/battle-simulator/personnel-decide.test.mjs` (two new regression tests, `makeMoraleState` gained an optional `prone` param). `tools/battle-simulator/analysis/audit-transcripts.log` is a real, regenerable artifact (`node tools/battle-simulator/analysis/transcript-audit.mjs`), covered by the repo's existing blanket `*.log` gitignore rule — not committed, same as the weight-search checkpoint. Full `battle-simulator` suite re-run clean after the fix.

## 54. Roster generalization sweep — the Archetype Ladder's biggest untested assumption

Ingram: "run the long term simulation now... use this as a chance to learn things through simulation, so feel free to make changes before this run." Every real number the whole Archetype Ladder has produced so far (§46-§53) was measured on exactly ONE roster — `lunar-17.json` + `security-goon.json`. This section is the first real check of whether any of it generalizes.

### A real bug, found before spending any overnight compute

Widened `transcript-audit.mjs` (§53) to three new roster pairs chosen for real mechanical diversity the default roster never exercises: `bennit.json`+`erabryth.json` (a real limited-use item, a real Stunned-inflicting conditionSave — Forced Reflection), `tough.json`+`xenowarden-greenhorn.json` (a real areaAttack, a second spellcaster matchup), `obozaya.json`+`kuriaku.json` (a plain, no-frills melee-only pair). Zero illegal proposals anywhere — but `idleTurnRate` came back suspiciously high on all three (6%-41%, versus 2-13% on the well-worn default roster). Traced directly: a downed-but-not-dead combatant's real dying/recovery-check turn (`engine.mjs`'s own `dying: true` entries — genuinely unable to act at all under the real Unconscious condition) was being counted as an idle turn by `metrics.mjs`, the same bug class Stunned was already excluded for, just never hit until a roster that actually produces real dying spirals got measured. Fixed the same way Stunned already was; re-ran all four rosters — `idleTurnRate` dropped to a flat 0.0% everywhere, including the default roster (whose own previously-reported idle numbers, e.g. §53's "cautious-v1 dropped from 5.4% to 1.9%," were themselves partly inflated by this same confound — the RELATIVE before/after comparison in §53 is still valid since both sides used the same metric consistently, but the absolute figures were higher than the real number).

### The real finding: baseline-v1 is NOT uniformly strong

Built `roster-generalization-sweep.mjs` (thin orchestration over the already-tested `runTournamentParallel`/`aggregateTournament`) and ran `baseline-v1` against every archetype and anchor baseline, n=24, on all three new rosters:

| Roster | Real losses for baseline-v1 (win rate) |
|---|---|
| `bennit`+`erabryth` | none — 54-100% across the board, roughly matching the default roster's own pattern |
| `tough`+`xenowarden-greenhorn` | **loses to `aggressive-v1` (25.0%)**, **loses to `zealot-v1` (33.3%)**, roughly breaks even vs. `brawler-v1` (41.7%) and `flanker-v1` (50.0%) |
| `obozaya`+`kuriaku` | **loses to `cautious-v1` (37.5%)**, **loses to `zealot-v1` (41.7%)** |

This directly contradicts the standing read from every earlier section: `baseline-v1` isn't a uniformly strong bot, it's a bot that happens to be strong on the one roster it's always been measured against. Full numbers appended to `battle-history.md` (2026-09-02, "Roster generalization sweep").

### A real mechanism, not just a number

Read a full transcript of `baseline-v1` losing to `aggressive-v1` on `tough`+`xenowarden-greenhorn` (seed 1), with real per-round HP tracking. Both sides trade evenly through round 2 — the party's own `tough` combatant even opens with a strong Frag Grenade against the weaker enemy. Round 3 is the whole game: `xenowarden-greenhorn` (opposition) Trips the party's `tough` combatant, then immediately Strikes it with Nano-edge Rapier while it's off-guard — and a SECOND opposition combatant's own strikes land on the same now-prone target later the same round, before it gets a chance to Stand. One real Trip, converted by two different attackers in the same round, swings 18 real damage onto a single 24-HP combatant in one round — decisive.
